\chapter{Exposé V. Local duality and the structure of the
H\^{}i(M)}\label{exposuxe9-v.-local-duality-and-the-structure-of-the-him}

\section{1. Complexes of
homomorphisms}\label{complexes-of-homomorphisms}

\textbf{1.1.} Let \(F\bullet\) and \(G\bullet\) be two graded modules;
then one writes

\[ \operatorname{Hom}\bullet(F\bullet, G\bullet) \]

for the graded module of homomorphisms of graded modules from
\(F\bullet\) into \(G\bullet\). Thus one has

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Homˢ(F•, G•) = ∏ₖ Hom(Fₖ, Gₖ₊ₛ).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Let \(F\bullet\) (resp. \(G\bullet\)) be a complex, and let \(d_{1}\)
(resp. \(d_{2}\)) be its differential; then for \(h \in
\operatorname{Hom}^{s}(F\bullet, G\bullet)\) one sets\footnote{\emph{N.D.E.}
  The original sign convention was different; but it is not compatible
  with the convention of Exposé VIII, which seems more reasonable, since
  in that case the cohomology in degree \texttt{0} is the set of
  homotopy classes of morphisms from \(F\bullet\) into \(G\bullet\). The
  calculations have been modified accordingly in what follows.}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{d(h) = h ∘ d₁ + (−1)\^{}\{s+1\} d₂ ∘ h.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

One verifies trivially that \(d \circ d = 0\), hence that
\(\operatorname{Hom}\bullet(F\bullet, G\bullet)\) equipped with \(d\) is
a complex. The cohomology group of this complex is written

\[ H\bullet(F\bullet, G\bullet). \]

If \(G\bullet\) is injective in each degree, then

\[ F\bullet \mapsto H\bullet(F\bullet, G\bullet) \]

is an exact ∂-functor. Likewise, for arbitrary \(F\bullet\),

\[ G\bullet \mapsto H\bullet(F\bullet, G\bullet) \]

is an exact δ-functor on the category of complexes \(G\bullet\) that are
injective in each degree.

\textbf{Remark 1.2.}

The cycles of \(\operatorname{Hom}\bullet(F\bullet, G\bullet)\) are the
homomorphisms from \(F\bullet\) into \(G\bullet\) that commute or
anticommute with the differentials, according to degree. The boundaries
of \(\operatorname{Hom}\bullet(F\bullet, G\bullet)\) are the
homomorphisms from \(F\bullet\) into \(G\bullet\) that are homotopic to
zero.

Let \(A\) be a ring, let \(M\) (resp. \(N\)) be an \(A\)-module, and let
\(R(M)\) (resp. \(R(N)\)) be an injective resolution of \(M\) (resp.
\(N\)). Then there exists a canonical isomorphism\footnote{\emph{N.D.E.}
  The strange original numbering has been preserved.}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Hˢ(R(M), R(N)) ≅ Extˢ(M, N).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Indeed, let \(i: M \to R(M)\) be the canonical augmentation, and let
\(h \in \operatorname{Hom}^{s}(R(M), R(N))\); one writes \(t_{s}\) for
the map

\[ h \mapsto h^{0} \circ i \]

from \(\operatorname{Hom}^{s}(R(M), R(N))\) into
\(\operatorname{Hom}(M, R(N)^{s})\). The family
\((t_{s})_{s\geqslant 0}\) defines a homomorphism of (ordinary)
complexes\footnote{\emph{N.D.E.} We still write \(M\) for the complex
  \texttt{M{[}0{]}} consisting of \(M\) placed in degree \texttt{0}.}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{t: Hom•(R(M), R(N)) → Hom•(M, R(N)),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

i.e.~one has \((dh)^{0} \circ i = d_{2} \circ h^{0} \circ i\).

One verifies easily that, upon passing to cohomology, \(t\) gives an
isomorphism. In particular, it follows that

\[ H\bullet(R(M), R(N)) \]

does not ``depend'' on the chosen injective resolution \(R(M)\) (resp.
\(R(N)\)) of \(M\) (resp. \(N\)).

To every exact sequence of \(A\)-modules

\[ 0 \to M' \to M \to M'' \to 0 \]

one associates an exact sequence of injective resolutions

\[ 0 \to R(M') \to R(M) \to R(M'') \to 0. \]

One verifies that the isomorphism (1.3) commutes with the homomorphisms

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Hˢ(R(M′), R(N)) → Hˢ⁺¹(R(M″), R(N)),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Extˢ(R(M′), R(N)) → Extˢ⁺¹(R(M″), R(N)),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

deduced from (6) and (5).

Let \(P\) be a third \(A\)-module, and let \(R(P)\) be an injective
resolution of \(P\); then composition of graded morphisms gives a
pairing

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Homⁱ(R(N), R(M)) × Homʲ(R(M), R(P)) → Homⁱ⁺ʲ(R(N), R(P)),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

which defines a pairing

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Hⁱ(R(N), R(M)) × Hʲ(R(M), R(P)) → Hⁱ⁺ʲ(R(N), R(P)),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

hence a homomorphism of functors in \(M\):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Hⁱ(R(N), R(M)) → Hom(Hʲ(R(M), R(P)), Hⁱ⁺ʲ(R(N), R(P))).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

We shall see that (1.4) is a homomorphism of δ-functors in \(M\). The
exact sequences (5) and (6) give a commutative diagram:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Homⁱ(R(N), R(M′))   ──→   Hom(Homʲ(R(M′), R(P)), Homⁱ⁺ʲ(R(N), R(P)))}
\NormalTok{       │                                       ↑}
\NormalTok{       │                                  Hom(q, id)}
\NormalTok{       ↓                                       │}
\NormalTok{Homⁱ(R(N), R(M))    ──→   Hom(Homʲ(R(M), R(P)), Homⁱ⁺ʲ(R(N), R(P)))}
\NormalTok{       │}
\NormalTok{       │ p}
\NormalTok{       ↓                                       │}
\NormalTok{Homⁱ(R(N), R(M″))   ──→   Hom(Homʲ(R(M″), R(P)), Homⁱ⁺ʲ(R(N), R(P))).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Let \(h \in \operatorname{Hom}^{i}(R(N), R(M''))\) (resp.
\(g \in \operatorname{Hom}^{j}(R(M'), R(P))\)) be a cycle, and let
\(h' \in \operatorname{Hom}^{i}(R(N), R(M))\) (resp.
\(g' \in \operatorname{Hom}^{j}(R(M), R(P))\)) be such that
\(p(h') = h\) (resp. \(q(g') = g\)); then to say that (1.4) is a
homomorphism of δ-functors in \(M\) is to say that

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{g ∘ dh′ − dg′ ∘ h}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

is a coboundary in \(\operatorname{Hom}\bullet(R(N), R(P))\).

Now one has

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{dh′ = h′ ∘ d₁ + (−1)\^{}\{i+1\} d₂ ∘ h′,}
\NormalTok{dg′ = g′ ∘ d₂ + (−1)\^{}\{j+1\} d₃ ∘ g′,}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

with the obvious notations. Hence (12) is written

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{g ∘ h′ ∘ d₁ + (−1)\^{}\{i+1\} g ∘ d₂ ∘ h′ − g′ ∘ d₂ ∘ h − (−1)\^{}\{j+1\} d₃ ∘ g′ ∘ h.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

On the other hand, since \(h\) and \(g\) are cycles, one has

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{g ∘ d₂ = (−1)ʲ d₃ ∘ g,}
\NormalTok{d₂ ∘ h = (−1)ⁱ h ∘ d₁,}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

hence, finally, (12) is written

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{d(g ∘ h′ + (−1)\^{}\{i+1\} g′ ∘ h),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

which completes the proof.

Thus (1.3) and (1.4) give a homomorphism of δ-functors in \(M\):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Extⁱ(N, M) → Hom(Extʲ(M, P), Extⁱ⁺ʲ(N, P)).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{2. The local duality theorem for a regular local
ring}\label{the-local-duality-theorem-for-a-regular-local-ring}

Let \(A\) be a regular local ring of dimension \(r\), let
\(\mathfrak{m}\) be the maximal ideal of \(A\), and let \(M\) be a
finitely generated \(A\)-module. One sets
\(H^{i}(M) = H^{i}_{\mathfrak{m}}(M)\) (hence \(H^{i}(M) = \lim\to
Ext^{i}(A/\mathfrak{m}^{k}, M)\)). One has seen (IV 5.4) that
\(I = H^{r}(A)\) is a dualizing module for \(A\); denote by \(D\) the
associated dualizing functor. In (1.5) setting
\(N = A/\mathfrak{m}^{k}\), \(P = A\), one obtains a homomorphism of
δ-functors in \(M\)

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{φₖ: Extⁱ(A/𝔪ᵏ, M) → Hom(Extʳ⁻ⁱ(M, A), Extʳ(A/𝔪ᵏ, A)).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Passing to the direct limit over \(k\), one finds a homomorphism of
δ-functors

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{φ: Hⁱ(M) → D(Extʳ⁻ⁱ(M, A)).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Theorem 2.1} (Local duality theorem)\textbf{.}

The functorial homomorphism \(\phi\) above is an isomorphism.

\emph{Proof.} If \(i > r\), the right-hand side of (14) is trivially
zero, and the left-hand side is zero because \(H^{i}(M) =
\lim\to_{k} Ext^{i}(A/\mathfrak{m}^{k}, M)\), and this holds for each
\(Ext^{i}(A/\mathfrak{m}^{k}, M)\) (syzygy theorem).

If \(i = r\), by what precedes, the two functors in \(M\), \(H^{r}(M)\)
and \(D(\operatorname{Hom}(M, A))\), are right exact; since \(A\) is
noetherian and \(M\) is finitely generated, it suffices to verify the
isomorphism for \(M = A\), which is immediate.

To show that \(\phi\) is a functorial isomorphism, it now suffices,
proceeding by descending induction on \(i\), to remark that every
finitely generated module admits a finite presentation, and that for
\(i < r\) the two sides of (14) are zero when \(M\) is finitely
generated free. This is evident for the right-hand side, and since
\(H^{i}\) commutes with finite sums it suffices, as for the left-hand
side, to show that \(H^{i}(A) = 0\) for \(i < r\). But this follows,
since \(prof(A) =
r\), from (III 3.4).

\section{3. Application to the structure of the
H\^{}i(M)}\label{application-to-the-structure-of-the-him}

\textbf{Theorem 3.1.}

Let \(A\) be a noetherian local ring, \(D\) a dualizing functor for
\(A\), and \(M\) a finitely generated \(A\)-module with \(M
\neq 0\), of dimension \(n\). Then one has:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{(\roman{enumi})}
\item
  \(H^{i}(M) = 0\) if \(i < 0\) or if \(i > n\).
\item
  \texttt{D(Hⁱ(M))\^{}} is a finitely generated module over \texttt{Â},
  of dimension \(\leqslant i\).
\item
  \(H^{n}(M) \neq 0\), and if \(A\) is complete, \(D(H^{n}(M))\) is of
  dimension \(n\) and
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Ass(D(Hⁿ(M))) = \{𝔭 ∈ Ass(M) | dim A/𝔭 = n\}.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\emph{Proof.} Let \(I\) be the dualizing module associated to \(D\). One
knows that \texttt{Î} is a dualizing module for \texttt{Â}. On the other
hand, one has

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Hⁱ(M)\^{} = Hⁱ(M̂),}
\NormalTok{D(Hⁱ(M))\^{} = Hom(Hⁱ(M̂), Î), and}
\NormalTok{dim M̂ = dim M;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

hence one may suppose \(A\) complete. Now, by a theorem of Cohen, every
complete local ring is a quotient of a regular local ring. To reduce to
that case, one needs the following lemma:

\textbf{Lemma 3.2.}

Let \(X\) (resp. \(Y\)) be a ringed space, let \(X'\) (resp. \(Y'\)) be
a closed subspace of \(X\) (resp. \(Y\)), and let \(f: X \to
Y\) be a morphism of ringed spaces such that \(f^{-1}(Y') = X'\). Let
\(F\) be an \(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module, and denote by \(A\) (resp.
\(B\)) the ring \(\Gamma(\mathcal{O}_{X})\) (resp.
\(\Gamma(\mathcal{O}_{Y})\)), and by \(f: B \to A\) the ring
homomorphism corresponding to \(f\). There exists a spectral sequence of
\(B\)-modules, with initial term

\[ E^{p,q}_{2} = H^{p}_{Y'}(Y, R^{g}f_{*}(F)), \]

abutting to the \(B\)-module \(H\bullet_{X'}(X, F)_{f}\).

\emph{Proof.} Let \(\mathcal{O}_{Y,Y'}\) be the sheaf
\(\mathcal{O}_{Y}|Y'\) extended by \texttt{0} outside \(Y'\) (see Exp.
I). One has an isomorphism of \(B\)-modules:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Hom(𝒪\_\{Y,Y′\}, f\_*(F)) ≅ Hom(f*(𝒪\_\{Y,Y′\}), F)\_[f].}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Now one has

\[ f*(\mathcal{O}_{Y,Y'}) = \mathcal{O}_{X,X'}, \]

and moreover if \(G\) is an injective \(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module, then
\(f_{*}(G)\) is an injective \(\mathcal{O}_{Y}\)-Module, at least if
\(f\) is flat --- a case to which one easily reduces by replacing
\(\mathcal{O}_{X}\), etc., by the constant sheaves of rings
\(\mathbb{Z}\). Hence the spectral sequence of the composite functor

\[ F \mapsto \operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{O}_{Y,Y'}, f_{*}(F)), \]

with initial term

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{E₂\^{}\{p,q\} = Extᵖ(Y; 𝒪\_\{Y,Y′\}, Rᵍf\_*(F)),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

abuts, taking into account (16) and (17), to

\[ Ext\bullet(X; \mathcal{O}_{X,X'}, F)_{f}. \]

The lemma then follows from (I 13 bis). QED

Let now \(f: B \to A\) be a surjective homomorphism of local rings. Let

\[ f: \operatorname{Spec}(A) \to \operatorname{Spec}(B) \]

be the corresponding morphism of affine schemes. Set
\(X = \operatorname{Spec}(A)\) (resp. \(X' = {\mathfrak{m}_{A}}\)), \(Y
= \operatorname{Spec}(B)\) (resp. \(Y' = {\mathfrak{m}_{B}}\)), and let
\(M\) be an \(A\)-module and \(\tilde{M}\) the corresponding
\(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module. Since \(R^{g}f_{*}(\tilde{M}) = 0\) for
\(q > 0\), the spectral sequence (15) degenerates, and by (3.2) one
obtains an isomorphism of \(B\)-modules:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Hⁿ\_\{\{𝔪\_B\}\}(Y, f\_*(M̃)) ≅ Hⁿ\_\{\{𝔪\_A\}\}(X, M̃)\_[f],}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

hence an isomorphism of \(B\)-modules:

\[ H^{n}_{\mathfrak{m}_{B}}(M_{f}) \cong H^{n}_{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}(M)_{f}. \]

On the other hand, if \texttt{D\_A} (resp. \texttt{D\_B}) is the
dualizing functor for \(A\) (resp. \(B\)), one has

\[ D_{A}(M)_{f} \cong D_{B}(M_{f}). \]

Finally, since one has a ring isomorphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{B/Ann M\_[f] ≅ A/Ann M,}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

one sees that the change of base rings under consideration changes
nothing. So suppose \(A\) is regular of dimension \(r\).

By (2.1) one has

\[ D(H^{i}(M)) = Ext^{r-i}(M, A). \]

We shall prove the equivalence of the following properties:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{(\alph{enumi})}
\item
  \(\dim Ext^{j}(M, A) \leqslant r - j\);
\item
  for every \(\mathfrak{p} \in X = \operatorname{Spec}(A)\) such that
  \(\dim A_{\mathfrak{p}} < j\), one has \(Ext^{j}(M,
  A)_{\mathfrak{p}} = 0\);
\item
  \(codim(Supp(Ext^{j}(M, A)), X) \geqslant j\).
\end{enumerate}

To prove (a) ⇒ (b), let \(\mathfrak{p} \in X\) with
\(\dim A_{\mathfrak{p}} < j\); then \(\dim A/\mathfrak{p} > r - j\),
hence by (a) \(Ann(Ext^{j}(M, A)) \not\subset \mathfrak{p}\), which
entails \(Ext^{j}(M, A)_{\mathfrak{p}} = 0\). Let
\(\mathfrak{p} \in Supp(Ext^{j}(M, A))\); then
\(Ext^{j}(M, A)_{\mathfrak{p}} \neq 0\), so by (b)
\(\dim A_{\mathfrak{p}}
\geqslant j\). Hence
\texttt{codim(Supp(Extʲ(M,\ A)),\ X)\ =\ inf\{dim\ A\_𝔭\ \textbar{}\ 𝔭\ ∈\ Supp(Extʲ(M,\ A))\}\ ⩾\ j},
that is, (b) ⇒ (c). Finally (c) implies (a) trivially.

Let us now prove the theorem.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{(\roman{enumi})}
\item
  Let \(x = (x_{1}, \cdots, x_{r})\) be a system of parameters for \(A\)
  such that \(x_{i} \in Ann M\) for \(i = 1, \cdots, r - n\). Let
  \(K\bullet((x^{k}), M)\) be the Koszul complex. One sees easily that
  the map \(K^{i}((x^{k}), M) \to K^{i}(({x^{k}}'), M)\) for \(k < k'\)
  is zero, if \(i > n\). It follows that
  \(H^{i}(M) = \lim\to H^{i}((x^{k}), M) = 0\) if \(i > n\). On the
  other hand, it is trivial that \(H^{i}(M) = 0\) if \(i < 0\), so (i)
  is proved.
\item
  Since \(A\) is regular, \(\dim A_{\mathfrak{p}} < j\) entails that the
  global homological dimension of \(A_{\mathfrak{p}}\) is strictly less
  than \(j\), and hence \(Ext^{j}(M, A)_{\mathfrak{p}} =
  Ext^{j}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}(M_{\mathfrak{p}}, A_{\mathfrak{p}}) = 0\);
  so one has proved (b) and consequently (a). (ii) then follows from
  (22) and from (a).
\item
  There exists a \(\mathfrak{p} \in Supp(M)\) such that
  \(\dim A_{\mathfrak{p}} = r - n\) and such that
  \(Supp(M_{\mathfrak{p}}) = {\mathfrak{m}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}}\). Since
  \(A_{\mathfrak{p}}\) is regular if \(A\) is, one finds
  \(prof A_{\mathfrak{p}} = r - n\), hence
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Extʳ⁻ⁿ\_A(M, A)\_𝔭 = Extʳ⁻ⁿ\_\{A\_𝔭\}(M\_𝔭, A\_𝔭) ≠ 0.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This implies, taking (22) into account, that on the one hand

\[ H^{n}(M) \neq 0, \]

and on the other hand

\[ \dim D(H^{n}(M)) \geqslant n, \]

hence by (ii)

\[ \dim D(H^{n}(M)) = n. \]

Let now \(Y = Supp(M)\). By (i) one knows that
\(D(H^{n}(M')) = Ext^{r-n}(M', A)\) is a functor in \(M'\), left exact,
on the category \((\mathcal{C}_{Y})^{\circ}\). Hence there exists an
\(A\)-module \(H\) and an isomorphism of functors in \(M'\):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Extʳ⁻ⁿ(M′, A) = Hom(M′, H).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Let \texttt{Yᵢ}, \(i = 1, \cdots, k\), be the irreducible components of
\(Y\) of maximum dimension. We shall see that the assertion
\(Ext^{r-n}(M', A) \neq 0\) is equivalent to the assertion: there exists
an \(i\) such that \(Supp M' \supset Y_{i}\). Indeed, if
\(Supp M' \supset Y_{i}\), then \(\dim(M') = n\), hence
\(Ext^{r-n}(M', A) \neq 0\).

If \(Supp M' \not\supset Y_{i}\) for every \(i = 1, \cdots, k\), then
\(\dim M' < n\) and

\[ D(H^{n}(M')) = Ext^{r-n}(M', A) = 0. \]

Since \texttt{Ass(Extʳ⁻ⁿ(M,\ A))\ =\ Supp\ M\ ∩\ Ass(H)}, one sees that
the last assertion of (iii) follows from the following lemma:

\textbf{Lemma 3.3.}

Let \(X = \operatorname{Spec}(A)\), let \(Y\) be a closed subset of
\(X\), let \(T: (\mathcal{C}_{Y})^{\circ} \to Ab\) be a left exact
functor, and let \texttt{Yᵢ}, \(i = 1, \cdots, k\), be a family of
irreducible components of \(Y\) such that the assertion: \(T(M) = 0\) is
equivalent to the assertion: \(\forall i, Supp M \not\supset Y_{i}\).
Then \(T\) is representable by a module \(H\) such that
\(Ass(H) = \bigcup^{k}_{i=1} {y_{i}}\), where \texttt{yᵢ} is the generic
point of \texttt{Yᵢ}, \(i = 1, \cdots, k\).

\emph{Proof.} Let \(y \in Y\); one constructs an \(A\)-module \(M(y)\)
such that \(Supp(M(y)) = {y}\). Suppose that \(y \neq y_{i}\) for every
\(i = 1, \cdots, k\); then \(Y_{i} \not\subset Supp(M(y))\) for every
\(i = 1, \cdots, k\), so \(T(M(y)) = 0\). It follows that

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Ass(T(M(y))) = Supp(M(y)) ∩ Ass(H) = ∅,}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

hence \(y \notin Ass(H)\). If \(y = y_{i}\), then
\(Y_{i} \subset Supp(M(y))\), so \(T(M(y)) \neq 0\), whence

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Ass(T(M(y))) = Supp(M(y)) ∩ Ass(H) ≠ ∅.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

By the first part of the proof, this implies \(y \in Ass(H)\), whence
the lemma. QED

\textbf{Example 3.4.}

Let \(A\) be a noetherian ring, let \(X = \operatorname{Spec}(A)\), and
let \(Y\) be a closed subset of \(X\) such that \(X - Y\) is affine;
then for every irreducible component \(Y_{\alpha}\) of \(Y\) one has
\(codim(Y_{\alpha}, X) \leqslant 1\).

Indeed, consider \(X\) as a prescheme over \(X\). Let
\(y_{\alpha} \in Y_{\alpha}\) be a generic point, and consider the
morphism \(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{X,y_{\alpha}}) \to X\). The
affine scheme obtained by base extension of \(X\) to
\(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{X,y_{\alpha}})\) is canonically
isomorphic to \(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{X,y_{\alpha}})\).

By
(\href{https://jcreinhold.github.io/ega/i/01-03-product-of-preschemes.html\#32-products-of-preschemes}{EGA
I 3.2.7}) one sees that if \(y_{0}\) is the unique closed point of
\(Y_{0} =
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{X,y_{\alpha}})\), then
\(Y_{0} - y_{0}\) is affine. By
(\href{https://jcreinhold.github.io/ega/iii/08-ch3-01-cohomology-affine-schemes.html\#13-cohomology-of-an-affine-scheme}{EGA
III 1.3.1}) one finds

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Hⁱ(Y₀ − y₀, 𝒪\_\{Y₀\}) = 0   if i \textgreater{} 0,}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

hence by (I 2.9)

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Hⁱ⁻¹(𝒪\_\{X,y\_α\}) = Hⁱ\_\{\{y₀\}\}(Y₀, 𝒪\_\{Y₀\}) = 0   if i ⩾ 2.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Taking 3.1 (iii) into account, it follows that

\[ \dim \mathcal{O}_{X,y_{\alpha}} \leqslant 1, \]

hence
\texttt{codim(Y\_α,\ X)\ =\ inf\_\{y\ ∈\ Y\_α\}\ dim\ 𝒪\_\{X,y\}\ ⩽\ 1}.
QED

Let \(A\) be a noetherian local ring, \(\mathfrak{m}\) its maximal
ideal, and \(M\) a finitely generated \(A\)-module. Suppose that \(A\)
is a quotient of a regular local ring. Set
\(X = \operatorname{Spec}(A)\), and for every \(x \in X\),
\(\mathfrak{m}_{x} = \mathfrak{m}A_{x}\).

\textbf{Proposition 3.5.}

The following two conditions are equivalent:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi})}
\item
  \(H^{i}(M)\) is of finite length;
\item
  \(\forall x \in X - {\mathfrak{m}}, H^{i-dim} {x}_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}(M_{x}) = 0\).
\end{enumerate}

\emph{Proof.} Taking (3.2) into account, we may suppose \(A\) regular.
By (2.1) we have

\[ H^{i}(M) = D(Ext^{r-i}(M, A)), \]

where \(r = \dim A\). By (IV 4.7), a) is equivalent\footnote{\emph{N.D.E.}
  Indeed, the point is to show that, \(E\) being a finitely generated
  \(A\)-module, if \(D(E)\) is of finite length then \(E\) is of finite
  length. Let \(K\) (resp. \(Q\)) be the kernel (resp. cokernel) of the
  canonical morphism \(\epsilon: E \to DD(E)\). The composition of
  \(D(\epsilon)\) and the canonical morphism \(\gamma: D(E) \to DDD(E)\)
  is the identity of \(D(E)\). Since \(D(E)\) is of finite length,
  \(\gamma\) is an isomorphism, and hence so is \(D(\epsilon)\). Since
  \(D\) is exact, it follows that \(D(K)\) and \(D(Q)\) are zero. It
  suffices to prove that if \(M\) is an \(A\)-module with zero dual,
  then \(M\) is zero, for one will then have \(E = DD(E)\) of finite
  length, just like \(D(E)\). Indeed, let \(M_{0}\) be a finitely
  generated submodule of \(M\). Since \(D\) is exact, \(D(M_{0})\) is a
  quotient of \(D(M)\), which is zero. Again by the exactness of \(D\),
  one has \(D(M_{0}/\mathfrak{m}_{A} M_{0}) = 0\), and hence, by
  biduality, the finite-length module \(M_{0}/\mathfrak{m}_{A} M_{0}\)
  is zero. Nakayama's lemma then ensures the vanishing of \(M_{0}\), and
  finally one obtains that of \(M\).} to

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Extʳ⁻ⁱ(M, A) is of finite length.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Now (24) is equivalent to

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{∀ x ∈ X − \{𝔪\}, one has Extʳ⁻ⁱ(M, A)\_x = 0.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

On the other hand \(A_{x}\) is regular of dimension \(r - \dim {x}\), so
by (2.1)

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Hⁱ⁻ᵈⁱᵐ \{x\}\_\{𝔪\_x\}(M\_x) = D(Ext\^{}\{(r − dim \{x\}) − (i − dim \{x\})\}\_\{A\_x\}(M\_x, A\_x)) = D(Extʳ⁻ⁱ\_\{A\_x\}(M\_x, A\_x)).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Since \(M\) is finitely generated, one has

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Extʳ⁻ⁱ\_A(M, A)\_x = Extʳ⁻ⁱ\_\{A\_x\}(M\_x, A\_x),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

whence the proposition.

\textbf{Corollary 3.6.}

In order that \(H^{i}(M)\) be of finite length for \(i \leqslant n\), it
is necessary and sufficient that

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{prof(M\_x) \textgreater{} n − dim \{x\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

for every \(x \in X - {\mathfrak{m}}\).

\emph{Proof.} Follows from (3.5) and (III 3.1).

\chapter{\texorpdfstring{Exposé VI. The functors
\(Ext^{\bullet}_{Z}(X; F, G)\) and
\(\mathcal{E}xt^{\bullet}_{Z}(F, G)\)}{Exposé VI. The functors Ext\^{}\{\textbackslash bullet\}\_\{Z\}(X; F, G) and \textbackslash mathcal\{E\}xt\^{}\{\textbackslash bullet\}\_\{Z\}(F, G)}}\label{exposuxe9-vi.-the-functors-extbullet_zx-f-g-and-mathcalextbullet_zf-g}

\section{1. Generalities}\label{generalities}

\subsection{1.1}\label{section}

Let \((X, \mathcal{O}_{X})\) be a ringed space and let \(Z\) be a
locally closed subset of \(X\). Let \(F\) and \(G\) be
\(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Modules; we denote by \(Ext^{i}_{Z}(X; F, G)\)
(resp. \(\mathcal{E}xt^{i}_{Z}(F, G)\)) the \(i\)-th derived functor of
the functor
\(G \mapsto \Gamma_{Z}(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(F, G))\)
(resp. \(G \mapsto \Gamma
Z(\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(F, G))\), where \(\Gamma Z\) denotes
the sheafified sections-with-support functor and
\(\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\) the sheafified \texttt{Hom}).

\textbf{Lemma.}

The sheaf \(\mathcal{E}xt^{i}_{Z}(F, G)\) is canonically isomorphic to
the sheaf associated with the presheaf \(U \mapsto
Ext^{i}_{Z \cap U}(U; F|U, G|U)\).

This follows from (\emph{Tôhoku}, 3.7.2) together with the fact that
\(\Gamma(U; \Gamma Z(\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(F,
G)))\) is canonically isomorphic to
\(\Gamma_{Z \cap U}(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}|U}(F|U, G|U))\).

\textbf{Theorem (Excision theorem).}

Let \(V\) be an open subset of \(X\) containing \(Z\). Then one has an
isomorphism of cohomological functors

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Ext\^{}•\_X(X; F, G) ≃ Ext\^{}•\_V(V; F|V, G|V).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Indeed, if \(G^{\bullet}\) is an injective resolution of \(G\), then
\(G^{\bullet}|V\) is an injective resolution of \(G|V\). The theorem
follows immediately.

\subsection{1.4}\label{section-1}

Let \(\mathcal{O}_{X,Z}\) be the \(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module defined by
the following conditions ({[}Godement{]}, 2.9.2):
\(\mathcal{O}_{X,Z}|_{X - Z} = 0\) and
\(\mathcal{O}_{X,Z}|_{Z} = \mathcal{O}_{X}|_{Z}\). We have seen that for
every \(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module \(H\) there is a functorial isomorphism
\(\Gamma_{Z}(H) \simeq
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X,Z}, H)\). From this
we deduce functorial isomorphisms in \(F\) and \(G\):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Γ\_Z(Hom\_\{𝒪\_X\}(F, G)) ≃ Hom\_\{𝒪\_X\}(𝒪\_\{X,Z\}, Hom\_\{𝒪\_X\}(F, G)),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Γ\_Z(Hom\_\{𝒪\_X\}(F, G)) ≃ Hom\_\{𝒪\_X\}(𝒪\_\{X,Z\} ⊗\_\{𝒪\_X\} F, G),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Γ\_Z(Hom\_\{𝒪\_X\}(F, G)) ≃ Hom\_\{𝒪\_X\}(F, Hom\_\{𝒪\_X\}(𝒪\_\{X,Z\}, G)) = Hom\_\{𝒪\_X\}(F, Γ\_Z(G)).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

It follows in particular from (1.4.2) that there is a
\(\partial\)-functorial isomorphism in \(F\) and \(G\)

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{θ: Ext\^{}i\_\{𝒪\_X\}(𝒪\_\{X,Z\} ⊗\_\{𝒪\_X\} F, G) ⥲ Ext\^{}i\_Z(X; F, G).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{1.5}\label{section-2}

By definition, the functor
\(G \mapsto \Gamma_{Z}(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(F, G))\) is
the composite of the functor
\(G \mapsto \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(F, G)\) and the functor
\(\Gamma_{Z}\). Since \(\Gamma_{Z}\) is left exact (I 1.9), since
\(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(F, G)\) is flasque whenever \(G\)
is injective, and since \(\Gamma_{Z}\) is exact on flasque sheaves (I
2.12), it follows from (\emph{Tôhoku}, 2.4.1) that there is a spectral
functor abutting to \(Ext^{\bullet}_{Z}(X; F, G)\) whose initial term is
\(H^{p}_{Z}(X, \mathcal{E}xt^{q}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(F,
G))\).

On the other hand, it follows from (1.4.3) that
\(\Gamma_{Z}(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(F, G))\) is the
composite of \(\Gamma_{Z}\) and the functor
\(H \mapsto \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(F, H)\).

Since the functor \(\Gamma_{Z}\) takes injectives to injectives (I 1.4),
it follows from (\emph{Tôhoku}, 2.4.1) that there is a spectral functor
abutting to \(Ext^{\bullet}_{Z}(X; F, G)\) whose initial term is
\(Ext^{p}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(X; F,
\mathcal{H}^{q}_{Z}(G))\).

It follows finally, from (1.4.2) and the spectral sequence for
\texttt{Ext}, that there is a spectral functor abutting to
\(Ext^{\bullet}_{Z}(X; F, G)\) whose initial term is
\(H^{p}(X; \mathcal{E}xt^{q}_{Z}(F, G))\). Whence the

\textbf{Theorem.}

There exist three spectral functors abutting to
\(Ext^{\bullet}_{Z}(X; F, G)\) whose initial terms are respectively

\[ H^{p}_{Z}(X, \mathcal{E}xt^{q}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(F, G)) \]

\[ H^{p}(X, \mathcal{E}xt^{q}_{Z}(F, G)) \]

\[ Ext^{p}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(X; F, \mathcal{H}^{q}_{Z}(G)). \]

\subsection{1.7}\label{section-3}

Let now \(Z'\) be a closed subset of \(Z\) and let \(Z'' = Z - Z'\). We
have an exact sequence

\[ 0 \to \mathcal{O}_{X,Z''} \to \mathcal{O}_{X,Z} \to \mathcal{O}_{X,Z'} \to 0 \]

which generalizes the exact sequence of ({[}Godement{]}, 2.9.3). This
exact sequence splits locally; hence for every
\(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module \(F\) we have a further exact sequence:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{0 → F ⊗\_\{𝒪\_X\} 𝒪\_\{X,Z″\} → F ⊗\_\{𝒪\_X\} 𝒪\_\{X,Z\} → F ⊗\_\{𝒪\_X\} 𝒪\_\{X,Z′\} → 0.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Let now \(G\) be an \(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module; applying the functor
\(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\bullet, G)\) to the exact
sequence (1.7.2), one deduces from (1.4.2) and the long exact sequence
for \texttt{Ext} the following theorem:

\textbf{Theorem.}

Let \(Z\) be a locally closed subset of \(X\), let \(Z'\) be a closed
subset of \(Z\), and let \(Z'' = Z - Z'\). Then there is an exact
sequence, functorial in \(F\) and \(G\):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{0 → Hom\_\{Z′\}(F, G) → Hom\_Z(F, G) → Hom\_\{Z″\}(F, G) → Ext\^{}1\_\{Z′\}(F, G) → ⋯}
\NormalTok{    ⋯ → Ext\^{}i\_Z(F, G) → Ext\^{}i\_\{Z″\}(F, G) → Ext\^{}\{i+1\}\_\{Z′\}(F, G) → ⋯}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Corollary.}

Let \(Y\) be a closed subset of \(X\) and let \(U = X - Y\). Then there
is an exact sequence, functorial in \(F\) and \(G\):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{0 → Hom\_Y(F, G) → Hom\_\{𝒪\_X\}(F, G) → Hom\_\{𝒪\_X|U\}(F|U, G|U) → Ext\^{}1\_Y(F, G) → ⋯}
\NormalTok{    ⋯ → Ext\^{}i\_\{𝒪\_X\}(F, G) → Ext\^{}i\_\{𝒪\_X|U\}(F|U, G|U) → Ext\^{}\{i+1\}\_Y(F, G) → ⋯}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This corollary is an immediate consequence of theorem (1.3) and theorem
(1.8).

\section{2. Applications to quasi-coherent sheaves on
preschemes}\label{applications-to-quasi-coherent-sheaves-on-preschemes}

\textbf{Proposition.}

Let \(X\) be a locally noetherian prescheme. For every locally closed
subset \(Z\) of \(X\), every coherent Module \(F\), and every
quasi-coherent Module \(G\) on \(X\), the sheaves
\(\mathcal{E}xt^{i}_{Z}(F, G)\) are quasi-coherent.

One shows, as in (1.6.3), that the Modules
\(\mathcal{E}xt^{i}_{Z}(F, G)\) are the abutment of a spectral sequence
with initial term
\(\mathcal{E}xt^{p}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(F, \mathcal{H}^{q}_{Z}(G))\). By
(II, cor. 3) the \(\mathcal{H}^{q}_{Z}(G)\) are quasi-coherent, and so
are the \(\mathcal{E}xt^{p}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(F,
\mathcal{H}^{q}_{Z}(G))\), since \(F\) is coherent. The proposition
follows immediately.

\subsection{2.2}\label{section-4}

Let now \(Y\) be a closed subprescheme of \(X\) and let \(\mathcal{I}\)
be a defining ideal of \(Y\). Let \(m\) and \(n\) be integers with
\(m \geqslant n \geqslant 0\); we denote by \(i_{n,m}\) the canonical
map \(\mathcal{O}_{Y_{m}} =
\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I}^{m+1} \to \mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I}^{n+1} = \mathcal{O}_{Y_{n}}\)
and by \(j_{n}\) the map \(\mathcal{O}_{X,Y} \to \mathcal{O}_{Y_{n}}\).
The system \((\mathcal{O}_{Y_{n}}, i_{n,m})\) forms a projective system,
and the maps \(j_{n}\) are compatible with the \(i_{n,m}\).

Applying the functor
\(Ext^{i}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(F \otimes \bullet, G)\), one deduces a
morphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{φ′: lim\_\{→ n\} Ext\^{}i\_\{𝒪\_X\}(X; F ⊗ 𝒪\_\{Y\_n\}, G) → Ext\^{}i\_\{𝒪\_X\}(X; F ⊗ 𝒪\_\{X,Y\}, G);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

this is a morphism of cohomological functors in \(G\). The morphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{φ: lim\_\{→ n\} Ext\^{}i\_\{𝒪\_X\}(X; F ⊗ 𝒪\_\{Y\_n\}, G) → Ext\^{}i\_Y(X; F, G)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

obtained as the composite of \(\phi'\) with \(\theta\) (cf.~1.4) is
therefore likewise a morphism of cohomological functors in \(G\).

One defines in the same way

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{φ̲: lim\_\{→ n\} ℰxt\^{}i\_\{𝒪\_X\}(F ⊗ 𝒪\_\{Y\_n\}, G) → ℰxt\^{}i\_Y(F, G).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Theorem.}

Let \(X\) be a locally noetherian prescheme, let \(Y\) be a closed
subset of \(X\) defined by a coherent ideal \(\mathcal{I}\), let \(F\)
be a coherent Module and let \(G\) be a quasi-coherent Module. Then:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi})}
\item
  \texttt{φ̲} is an isomorphism.
\item
  If \(X\) is noetherian, \(\phi\) is an isomorphism.
\end{enumerate}

The proof of b) being almost word for word that of (II 6 b)), thanks to
the spectral sequence 1.6.2, we shall not reproduce it.

For the proof of a), one may, by (2.1), assume \(X\) affine with ring
\(A\), \(F\) (resp. \(G\)) defined by an \(A\)-module \(M\) (resp.
\(N\)), and \(\mathcal{I}\) by an ideal \(I\). It suffices to prove that
the homomorphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{lim\_\{→ n\} Ext\^{}i\_A(M/I\^{}n M, N) → Ext\^{}i\_Y(X, F, G)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

deduced from \texttt{φ̲} is an isomorphism.

Indeed, for \(i = 0\), one can canonically identify both sides of
(2.3.1) with the submodule of \(\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(M,
N)\) consisting of those elements of \(\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(M, N)\)
annihilated by some power of \(I\). One then sees that the homomorphism
(2.3.1) is none other than the identity map.

The functor \(N \mapsto \lim_{\to n} Ext^{\bullet}_{A}(M/I^{n} M, N)\)
is a universal \(\partial\)-functor. We shall show that the same holds
for the functor \(N \mapsto Ext^{\bullet}_{Y}(M, N)\). Indeed, if \(N\)
is an injective module, by (9 and 11), \(\mathcal{H}^{q}_{Y}(N) = 0\)
for \(q \neq 0\); and by (IV.2.2), \(\mathcal{H}^{0}_{Y}(N)\) is
injective.

It follows then that
\(Ext^{p}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(X; M, \mathcal{H}^{q}_{Y}(N)) = 0\) for
\(p + q \neq 0\); hence, by (1.6.3), \(Ext^{i}_{Y}(M, N) = 0\) for
\(i \neq 0\) and \(N\) injective. This completes the proof.

\section{Bibliography}\label{bibliography-1}

Same references as those listed at the end of Exp. I, cited respectively
{[}\emph{Tohoku}{]} and {[}Godement{]}.

\chapter{Exposé VII. Vanishing criteria and coherence conditions for the
sheaves ℰxt\^{}i\_Y(F,
G)}\label{exposuxe9-vii.-vanishing-criteria-and-coherence-conditions-for-the-sheaves-ux2130xti_yf-g}

\section{\texorpdfstring{1. Study for
\(i < n\)}{1. Study for i \textless{} n}}\label{study-for-i-n}

We prove a lemma.

\textbf{Lemma.}

Let \(X\) be a locally noetherian prescheme, \(Y\) a closed subset of
\(X\), and \(G\) a quasi-coherent \(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module. Suppose
that for every coherent \(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module \(F\) with support
contained in \(Y\), one has

\[ \mathcal{E}xt^{n-1}(F, G) = 0. \]

Then for every coherent \(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module \(F\) and every
closed subset \(Z\) of \(X\) such that \(Y \supset Supp F \cap
Z\), one has

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ℰxt\^{}n\_Z(F, G) ≅ ℋom(F, ℋ\^{}n\_Y(G)).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

We first remark that

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ℰxt\^{}i\_Z(F, G) = ℰxt\^{}i\_\{Z ∩ Supp F\}(F, G)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

(trivial, cf.~Exposé VI). We first carry out the proof for \(Z = X\), so
that \(Supp F \subset Y\). The functor

\[ F \mapsto \mathcal{E}xt^{n}(F, G), \]

defined on the category of coherent \(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Modules with
support contained in \(Y\), is left exact. By (IV 1.3), it is
represented by

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{I = lim\_\{→ k\} ℰxt\^{}n(𝒪\_X/𝓘\^{}\{k+1\}, G),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

where \(\mathcal{I}\) is the ideal of definition of \(Y\). Now, by (II
6), one knows that

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ℋ\^{}n\_Y(G) ≅ lim\_\{→ k\} ℰxt\^{}n(𝒪\_X/𝓘\^{}\{k+1\}, G).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Whence the conclusion when \(Z = X\). Still by (VI 2.3), one knows that

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ℰxt\^{}n\_Z(F, G) ≅ lim\_\{→ k\} ℰxt\^{}n(F/𝓙\^{}\{k+1\}F, G),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

where \(\mathcal{J}\) is the ideal of definition of \(Z\). The support
of \(F/\mathcal{J}^{k+1}F\) is contained in \(Y\) whenever
\(Z \cap Supp F \subset Y\); by what we have just proved, we therefore
have

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ℰxt\^{}n\_Z(F, G) ≅ lim\_\{→ k\} ℋom(F/𝓙\^{}\{k+1\}F, ℋ\^{}n\_Y(G)).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

It remains to show that the natural homomorphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{lim\_\{→ k\} ℋom(F/𝓙\^{}\{k+1\}F, ℋ\^{}n\_Y(G)) → ℋom(F, ℋ\^{}n\_Y(G))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

is an isomorphism when \(Z \cap Supp F \subset Y\). Now \(X\) can be
covered by noetherian affine open sets; one is thus reduced to the case
where \(X\) is noetherian affine. Then \(F(X)\) is a finitely generated
\(\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)\)-Module and
\(Supp \mathcal{H}^{n}_{Y}(G) \subset Y\). Hence every homomorphism
\(u: F(X) \to \mathcal{H}^{n}_{Y}(G)(X)\) is annihilated by a power of
\(\mathcal{I}\), and therefore by a power of \(\mathcal{J}\). QED.

\textbf{Proposition.}

Let \(X\) be a locally noetherian prescheme, \(Y\) a closed subset of
\(X\), \(G\) a quasi-coherent \(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module, and \(n\) an
integer. For any closed subsets \(Z\) and \(S\) of \(X\) such that
\(Z \cap S = Y\), the following conditions are equivalent:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \texttt{ℋ\^{}i\_Y(G)\ =\ 0} for \texttt{i\ \textless{}\ n};
\item
  there exists a coherent \texttt{𝒪\_X}-Module \texttt{F}, of support
  \texttt{S}, such that

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ℰxt\^{}i\_Z(F, G) = 0 for i \textless{} n;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}
\item
  for every coherent \texttt{𝒪\_X}-Module \texttt{F} with support
  contained in \texttt{S} (i.e.

  \(Supp F \cap Z = Supp F \cap Y\)), one has

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ℰxt\^{}i\_Z(F, G) = 0 for i \textless{} n;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}
\item
  for every coherent \texttt{𝒪\_X}-Module \texttt{F}, one has

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ℰxt\^{}i\_Y(F, G) = 0 for i \textless{} n.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}
\end{enumerate}

Moreover, if these conditions hold, then for every coherent
\(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module \(F\) and every closed subset \(Z'\) of \(X\)
such that \(Z' \cap Supp F = Y \cap Supp F\), one has isomorphisms

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ℰxt\^{}n\_Z(F, G) ≅ ℰxt\^{}n\_Y(F, G) ≅ ℋom(F, ℋ\^{}n\_Y(G)).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\emph{Proof.} We argue by induction. The proposition is trivial for
\(n < 0\). Suppose it has been proved for \(n < q\). If one of the
conditions holds for \(n = q\), and for two subsets \(Z\) and \(S\) as
stated, then by the induction hypothesis we have, for every closed
subset \(Z'\) of \(X\) and every coherent \(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module
\(F\) such that \(Z' \cap Supp F = Y
\cap Supp F\), isomorphisms

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ℰxt\^{}\{q−1\}\_\{Z\textquotesingle{}\}(F, G) ≅ ℋom(F, ℋ\^{}\{q−1\}\_Y(G)) ≅ ℰxt\^{}\{q−1\}\_Y(F, G).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Hence:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \begin{enumerate}
  \def\labelenumi{(\roman{enumi})}
  \tightlist
  \item
    ⇒ (iv), by taking \(Z' = Y\) in (1.1);
  \end{enumerate}
\item
  \begin{enumerate}
  \def\labelenumi{(\roman{enumi})}
  \setcounter{enumi}{3}
  \tightlist
  \item
    ⇒ (iii), by taking \(Z' = Z\) in (1.1);
  \end{enumerate}
\item
  \begin{enumerate}
  \def\labelenumi{(\roman{enumi})}
  \setcounter{enumi}{2}
  \tightlist
  \item
    ⇒ (ii), by taking \(F = \mathcal{O}_{S}\);
  \end{enumerate}
\item
  \begin{enumerate}
  \def\labelenumi{(\roman{enumi})}
  \setcounter{enumi}{1}
  \tightlist
  \item
    ⇒ (i), by taking \(Z' = Z\) in (1.1); this gives
    \(\mathcal{H}om(F, \mathcal{H}^{q-1}_{Y}(G)) = 0\). One then remarks
    that
  \end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Supp ℋ\^{}\{q−1\}\_Y(G) ⊂ Y = Z ∩ S ⊂ S = Supp F,}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

  and one applies the following lemma:
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Lemma.}

Let \(X\) be a prescheme, let \(P\) be a coherent
\(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module, and let \(H\) be a quasi-coherent
\(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module such that

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ℋom(P, H) = 0 and Supp P ⊃ Supp H.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Then \(H = 0\).

It suffices to prove the lemma when \(X\) is affine, since the affine
open sets form a base of the topology of \(X\) and the hypotheses are
preserved by restriction to an open set.

Now in that case one is reduced to a problem on \(A\)-modules, where
\(X = \operatorname{Spec}(A)\). One applies the formula (valid under the
sole hypothesis that \(M\) is of finite type)

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Ass Hom\_A(P, H) = Supp P ∩ Ass H.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

One knows that \texttt{Ass\ H\ ⊂\ Supp\ H\ ⊂\ Supp\ P} and that
\(Ass \operatorname{Hom}_{A}(P, H) = \emptyset\); hence \(Ass H =
\emptyset\), so \(H = 0\).

To complete the proof of the proposition, it remains to observe that
(iv) allows us to apply 1.1.

\textbf{Corollary.}

Let \(G\) be a coherent Cohen-Macaulay \(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module, and
let \(n \in \mathbb{Z}\). The conditions of 1.2 are equivalent to:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{codim(Y ∩ Supp G, Supp G) ⩾ n.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}
\end{enumerate}

Recall first that an \(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-module is said to be
Cohen-Macaulay if, for every \(x \in X\), the stalk \(G_{x}\) is a
Cohen-Macaulay \(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\)-module, i.e.~one has for every
\(x \in S = Supp G\):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{prof G\_x = dim G\_x = dim 𝒪\_\{S,x\}.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

By Proposition III 3.3, condition (i) of 1.2 is equivalent to

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{prof\_Y G = inf\_\{x ∈ Y\} prof G\_x ⩾ n,}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

and therefore also to

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{prof\_Y G = inf\_\{x ∈ Y ∩ S\} prof G\_x ⩾ n,}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

since the depth of a zero module is infinite. Now, by definition,

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{codim(Y ∩ S, S) = inf\_\{x ∈ S ∩ Y\} dim 𝒪\_\{S,x\},}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

whence the conclusion, by applying formula (1.2).

We shall now prove a result that lets us deduce the coherence conditions
we have in view from certain vanishing criteria.

\textbf{Lemma.}

Let \(X\) be a locally noetherian prescheme. Let \(T^{\bullet}\) be an
exact contravariant \(\partial\)-functor, defined on the category of
coherent \(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Modules, with values in the category of
\(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Modules. Let \(Y\) be a closed subset of \(X\). Let
\(i \in \mathbb{Z}\). Suppose that, for every coherent
\(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module with support contained in \(Y\), \(T^{i} F\)
and \(T^{i-1} F\) are coherent. Let \(F\) be a coherent
\(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module. For \(T^{i} F\) to be coherent, it is
necessary and sufficient that \(T^{i} F''\) be coherent, where we have
set

\[ F'' = F/\Gamma_{Y}(F). \]

Indeed, \(F' = \Gamma_{Y}(F)\) is coherent because \(X\) is locally
noetherian; the cohomology exact sequence of \(T^{\bullet}\) then gives

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{T\^{}\{i−1\} F\textquotesingle{} → T\^{}i F\textquotesingle{}\textquotesingle{} → T\^{}i F → T\^{}i F\textquotesingle{},}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

where the outer terms are coherent, whence the conclusion.

\textbf{Lemma.}

If \(F\) and \(G\) are coherent, and if \(Supp F \subset Y\), then
\(\mathcal{E}xt^{i}_{Y}(F, G)\) is coherent.

Indeed, \(\mathcal{E}xt^{i}_{Y}(F, G)\) is isomorphic to
\(\mathcal{E}xt^{i}(F, G)\); this is valid, moreover, on any ringed
space \(X\): if \(Z\) is a closed subset containing \(Y \cap Supp F\),
then \(\mathcal{E}xt^{i}_{Z}(F, G)\) is isomorphic to
\(\mathcal{E}xt^{i}_{Y}(F, G)\) (cf.~Exposé VI).

\textbf{Proposition.}

Suppose \(F\) and \(G\) are coherent, and set \(S = Supp F\),
\(S' = S \cap (X - Y)\). Suppose that, for every \(x \in Y \cap
S'\), one has \(prof G_{x} \geqslant n\). Then
\(\mathcal{E}xt^{i}_{Y}(F, G)\) is coherent for \(i < n\).

Indeed, 1.6 allows us to apply 1.5 to
\(T^{\bullet}(F) = \mathcal{E}xt^{\bullet}_{Y}(F, G)\). Setting \(F'' =
F/\Gamma_{Y}(F)\), one sees that \(Supp F'' = S'\). Now, by III 3.3, the
hypothesis on the depth of \(G\) ensures the vanishing of
\(\mathcal{H}^{i}_{Y \cap S'}(G)\) for \(i < n\); by 1.2, one deduces
the vanishing of \(T^{i} F''\) for \(i < n\), whence the conclusion by
1.5.

\section{\texorpdfstring{2. Study for
\(i > n\)}{2. Study for i \textgreater{} n}}\label{study-for-i-n-1}

Let \(X\) be a locally noetherian regular prescheme, that is, one all of
whose local rings are regular. Let \(Y\) be a closed subset of \(X\).
Let \(F\) and \(G\) be two coherent \(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Modules. Set
\(S = Supp F\), \(S' = S \cap (X -
Y)\). Set

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{m = sup\_\{x ∈ Y ∩ S\} dim 𝒪\_\{X,x\},}
\NormalTok{n = sup\_\{x ∈ Y ∩ S\textquotesingle{}\} dim 𝒪\_\{X,x\};}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

one has \(n \leqslant m\).

\textbf{Proposition.}

In the situation just described, one has:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\mathcal{E}xt^{i}_{Y}(F, G) = 0\) for \(i > m\),
\item
  \(\mathcal{E}xt^{i}_{Y}(F, G)\) is coherent for \(i > n\).
\end{enumerate}

Note first that \(\mathcal{E}xt^{i}_{Y}(F, G)\) is coherent for every
\(i\) when \(Supp F \subset Y\). Moreover, setting \(F''
= F/\Gamma_{Y}(F)\) as above, one sees that \(Supp F'' = S'\), so that
(2) follows from (1) and from 1.3.

To prove (1), one first remarks that

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ℰxt\^{}i\_Y(F, G) ≅ lim\_\{→ k\} ℰxt\^{}i(F/𝓙\^{}k F, G),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

where \(\mathcal{J}\) is the ideal of definition of \(Y\). On the other
hand, it follows from Theorem 4.2.2 of (A. Grothendieck, ``Sur quelques
points d'algèbre homologique'', \emph{Tôhoku Mathematical Journal}
\textbf{9} (1957), pp.~119--221) that the Ext sheaves commute with the
formation of stalks, at least when \(X\) is a locally noetherian
prescheme and the first argument is coherent; since the same is true of
direct limits, one finds isomorphisms

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(ℰxt\^{}i\_Y(F, G))\_x ≅ lim\_\{→ k\} Ext\^{}i\_\{𝒪\_\{X,x\}\}((F/𝓙\^{}k F)\_x, G\_x)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

for every \(x \in X\). Since
\(Supp \mathcal{E}xt^{i}_{Y}(F, G) \subset S \cap Y\), to conclude it
suffices to remark that \(x \in Y \cap S\) entails
\(\dim \mathcal{O}_{X,x} \leqslant m\), hence

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Ext\^{}i\_\{𝒪\_\{X,x\}\}((F/𝓙\^{}k F)\_x, G\_x) = 0 for i \textgreater{} m,}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

since the global cohomological dimension of a regular local ring is
equal to its dimension.\footnote{Cf.
  \href{https://jcreinhold.github.io/ega/iv/04-ch0-17-regular-rings.html\#173-cohomological-theory-of-regular-rings}{EGA
  0\_IV 17.3.1}.}

Let \(X\) be a locally noetherian prescheme; for every subset \(P\) of
\(X\), set

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{D(P) = \{ dim 𝒪\_\{X,p\} | p ∈ P \}.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Lemma.}

If \(P\) is the underlying space of a connected subprescheme of \(X\),
then \(D(P)\) is an interval.

Indeed, let \(\alpha\) and \(\beta\) belong to \(D(P)\), corresponding
to points \(p\) and \(q\) of \(P\). We show that there exists a sequence
of points of \(P\), \((p = p_{1}, \cdots, p_{n} = q)\), such that for
\(1 \leqslant i < n\) one has \(|\dim
\mathcal{O}_{X,p_{i}} - \dim \mathcal{O}_{X,p_{i+1}}| = 1\); it will
follow that \(D(P)\) contains the interval \([\alpha,
\beta]\). For this, one remarks that \(p\) and \(q\) can be joined by a
chain of irreducible components of \(P\) such that two successive
components meet. One is reduced to the case where \(p\) is the generic
point of an irreducible component \(Q\) of \(P\), and where \(q \in Q\),
and so \(q \supset p\) as ideals of \(\mathcal{O}_{q}\), where the
assertion is trivial from the definition of dimension.

\textbf{Proposition.}

Let \(X\) be a locally noetherian regular prescheme, \(Y\) a closed
subset of \(X\), and \(F\) a coherent \(\mathcal{O}_{X}\)-Module. Let
\(P = Y \cap Supp F \cap (X - Y)\). Let \(n \in \mathbb{Z}\), and
suppose that \(n \notin
D(P)\). Then \(\mathcal{E}xt^{n}_{Y}(F, \mathcal{O}_{X})\) is coherent.

The conclusion is local and the hypotheses are preserved by restriction
to an open set. Now \(P\) is closed and so locally noetherian, hence
locally connected; we may therefore assume \(X\) affine and noetherian,
and \(P\) connected. Set \texttt{D(P)\ =\ {[}a,\ b{[}}, which is
legitimate by the preceding lemma. If \(n > b\), we conclude by 2.1; if
\(n < a\), then \(n < \dim
\mathcal{O}_{X,x} = prof \mathcal{O}_{X,x}\) for every \(x \in P\), and
we conclude by 1.7.
