which is none other than the relation (∗∗); one has therefore proved the
equivalence

\[ (ii)_{t} \Leftrightarrow (\ast\ast\ast). \]

We are going to transform the relation (∗∗∗); one has first

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i(U′\_1, DF′) = H\^{}i(R f′\_\{1*\}(DF′\_1))\_\{t′\};}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

but by (SGA 5 I 1.12), there exists a canonical isomorphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{R f′\_\{1*\}(DF′\_1) ≃ D(R\^{}! f′\_1(F′\_1)),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

where \(D(R^{!} f'_{1}(F'_{1}))\) denotes the dual of
\(R^{!} f'_{1}(F'_{1})\) with respect to \(K\). One sees thus that
(ii)\_t is equivalent to

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(H\^{}i(D(R\^{}! f′\_1(F′\_1))))\_\{t′\} = 0 for i \textgreater{} −n.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Applying again the local duality theorem (SGA 5 I 4.5.3), but this time
at the point \(t'\), one finds

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i(D(R\^{}! f′\_1(F′\_1)))\_\{t′\} ≃ D(H\^{}\{−i\}\_\{t′\}(R\^{}! f′\_1(F′\_1))),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

and finally (ii)\_t is equivalent to the relation

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i\_\{t′\}(R\^{}! f′\_1(F′\_1)) = 0 for i \textless{} n,}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

that is, \(prof_{t'}(R^{!} f'_{1}(F'_{1})) \geqslant n\), which
completes the proof of the theorem.

\textbf{Remark 4.2.3.}

The reasoning simplifies considerably when one supposes that \(S\)
admits (at least locally) a dualizing complex (for example is locally
immersible in a regular scheme). This avoids recourse to a completion
(the passage to the strictly local case being immediate), to 2.3.3, and
to the rather unpleasant technical statement 3.6, which one can then
replace by the more sympathetic reference 3.7.

\textbf{Corollary 4.3.}

Let \(S\) be an excellent scheme of characteristic zero and
\(f : U \to S\) a separated morphism of finite type, such that \(U\) is
the union of \(c + 1\) open sets, affine over \(S\). Let \(F\) be a
complex of sheaves of \(\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}\)-modules, bounded below
with constructible cohomology, \(n\) an integer, and \(T\) a closed part
of \(S\). Suppose that, for every point \(u \in U\), one has

\[ prof_{u}(F) \geqslant n - \delta_{T}(u). \]

Then one has

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{prof\_T(R\^{}! f(F)) ⩾ n − c.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Let indeed \(U_{j}\), \(0 \leqslant j \leqslant c\), be a covering of
\(U\) by open sets \(U_{j}\) affine over \(S\). Resuming the notations
of the proof of 4.2, one has, for every \(j\),

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i(U′\_j, H\^{}q(DF′)) = 0 for i \textgreater{} −n.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Using the spectral sequence relating the cohomology of \(U\) to that of
the covering formed by the \(U_{j}\) (SGA 4 V 2.4), the preceding
relation shows that one has

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i(U′, H\^{}q(DF′)) = 0 for i \textgreater{} −n + c.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The corollary follows from the end of the proof of 4.2.

\textbf{Corollary 4.4.}

Let \(S\) be an excellent scheme of characteristic zero, \(g : X \to S\)
a morphism, \(U\) an open set of \(X\), union of \(c +
1\) opens affine over \(S\), \(Y\) a closed subscheme with underlying
space \(X - U\), and \(j : Y \to X\) the natural morphism. Let \(F\) be
a complex of sheaves of \(\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}\)-modules on \(X\),
bounded below with constructible cohomology, \(T\) a closed part of
\(S\), and \(n\) an integer. Suppose that, for every point \(u\) of
\(U\), one has

\[ prof_{u}(F) \geqslant n - \delta_{T}(u). \]

Then the canonical morphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i\_\{g\^{}\{−1\}(T)!\}(X/S, F) → H\^{}i\_\{(g\^{}\{−1\}(T) ∩ Y)!\}(Y/S, j\^{}* F)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

is bijective for \(i < n - c - 1\), injective for \(i = n - c - 1\).

This follows immediately from 4.1 and 4.3.

\textbf{Corollary 4.5} (Local Lefschetz theorem).

Let \(S\) be an excellent henselian local scheme of characteristic zero,
\(t\) the closed point of \(S\), \(X\) a scheme proper over
\(S' = S - {t}\), and \(U\) an open set of \(X\), union of \(c + 1\)
affine opens. Let \(Y\) be a closed subscheme of \(X\) with underlying
space \(X - U\), \(j : Y \to X\) the canonical morphism, \(F\) a complex
of sheaves of \(\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}\)-modules on \(X\), bounded below
with constructible cohomology, and \(n\) an integer. Suppose that, for
every point \(u\) of \(U\), one has

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{prof\_u(F) ⩾ n − δ\_\{t′\}(u), where δ\_\{t′\}(u) = δ\_t(u) − 1.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Then the canonical morphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i(X, F) → H\^{}i(Y, j\^{}* F)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

is bijective for \(i < n - c - 1\), injective for \(i = n - c - 1\).

Let \(f : U \to S\) be the canonical morphism; it follows from 4.2,
applied by replacing \(n\) by \(n + 1\), that one has

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{prof\_t(R\^{}! f(F|\_U)) ⩾ n + 1 − c.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The preceding relation shows that the canonical morphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i(S, R\^{}! f(F|\_U)) → H\^{}i(S′, R\^{}! f(F|\_U))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

is bijective for \(i < n - c\), injective for \(i = n - c\). Since
\(R^{!} f(F|_{U})\) is null outside \(S'\), one has \(H^{i}(S,
R^{!} f(F|_{U})) \simeq H^{i}(R^{!} f(F|_{U}))_{t} = 0\), and
consequently

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(∗) H\^{}i(S′, R\^{}! f(F|\_U)) = 0 for i \textless{} n − c.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Let \(g : X \to S'\), \(h : Y \to S'\), \(f' : U \to S'\) be the
canonical morphisms. It follows from the distinguished triangle

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{              R h\_*(j\^{}* F)}
\NormalTok{             ↗            ↘}
\NormalTok{   R\^{}! f′(F|\_U) ←{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-} R g\_*(F)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

that condition (∗) is equivalent to the fact that the morphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i(S′, R g\_*(F)) → H\^{}i(S′, R h\_*(j\^{}* F))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

is bijective for \(i < n - c - 1\), injective for \(i = n - c - 1\).
Since this morphism is canonically identified with the morphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i(X, F) → H\^{}i(Y, j\^{}* F),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

the conclusion follows immediately.

\textbf{Corollary 4.6} (Global Lefschetz theorem).

Let \(S\) be the spectrum of a field, \(X\) a scheme proper over \(S\),
and \(U\) an open set of \(X\) union of \(c + 1\) affine opens. Let
\(Y\) be a closed subscheme of \(X\) with underlying space \(X - U\),
\(j : Y \to X\) the canonical morphism, \(F\) a complex of sheaves of
\(\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}\)-modules on \(X\), bounded below with
constructible cohomology, and \(n\) an integer. Suppose that, for every
point \(u\) of \(U\), one has

\[ prof_{u}(F) \geqslant n - \dim({u}). \]

Then the canonical morphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i(X, F) → H\^{}i(Y, j\^{}* F)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

is bijective for \(i < n - c - 1\), injective for \(i = n - c - 1\).

More generally, if \(g : X \to S\) is a separated morphism of finite
type, the hypotheses on \(S\), \(U\), \(Y\), \(F\) being the same as
before, then the canonical morphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i\_!(X/S, F) → H\^{}i\_!(Y/S, j\^{}* F)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

(where \(H^{i}_{!}\) denotes cohomology with proper support, that is,
\(H^{i}_{!}(X/S, F) = H^{i}(S, R^{!} g(F))\)) is bijective for
\(i < n - c - 1\), injective for \(i = n - c - 1\).

The corollary is a particular case of 4.4, with \(T = S\).

Here is a partial converse to 4.3:

\textbf{Proposition 4.7.}

Let \(S\) be a noetherian scheme, \(f : U \to S\) a morphism of finite
type. Suppose that there exists a dualizing complex \(K\) on \(S\) and
that \(R^{!} f(K)\) is a dualizing complex on \(U\). Let \(T\) be a
closed part of \(S\) and \(c\) an integer. Then the following conditions
are equivalent:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{(\roman{enumi})}
\tightlist
\item
  For every complex of sheaves of \(\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}\)-modules
  \(F\) on \(U\), bounded below with constructible cohomology, and for
  every integer \(n\) such that, for every point \(u\) of \(U\),
\end{enumerate}

\[ prof_{u}(F) \geqslant n - \delta_{T}(u), \]

one has

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{prof\_T(R\^{}! f(F)) ⩾ n − c.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{(\roman{enumi})}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  For every constructible sheaf of \(\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}\)-modules
  \(G\) on \(U\) and for every point \(t \in T\), one has
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(R\^{}p f\_*(G))\_t = 0 for p \textgreater{} δ(G, f, t) + c}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

(let us recall from (SGA 4 XIX 6.0) that
\(\delta(G, f, t) = \sup{\delta_{t}(u) | t \in {u} and G_{u} \neq 0}\)).

N.B. Condition (ii) is satisfied by virtue of 3.1 if \(f\) is separated
and if \(U\) is, locally on \(S\) for the étale topology, a union of
\(c + 1\) opens affine over \(S\), so 4.7 contains 4.3.\footnote{At
  least in the case where \(S\) admits locally a dualizing complex, for
  example \(S\) locally immersible in a regular scheme.}

One can evidently suppose that \(S\) is local and that \(T\) is the
closed point \(t\) of \(S\).

The proof of (ii) ⇒ (i) is essentially identical to part 2°) of the
proof of 4.2. Let us show briefly that (i) ⇒ (ii). The local duality
theorem (SGA 5 I 4.3.2) applied to \texttt{DG} shows that

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{D H\^{}i\_u(DG) ≃ H\^{}\{−i − 2 δ\_t(u)\}(G)\_u.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Since \(G\) is reduced to degree \texttt{0}, one therefore has
\(H^{i}_{u}(DG) = 0\) except perhaps for \(i = -2 \delta_{t}(u)\); more
precisely

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{prof\_u(DG) = \{ −2 δ\_t(u)  if G\_u ≠ 0,}
\NormalTok{              \{ ∞          if G\_u = 0.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

It follows that one has, whatever \(u \in U\):

\[ prof_{u}(DG) \geqslant -n - \delta_{t}(u). \]

It then follows from hypothesis (i) that one has
\(prof_{t}(R^{!} f(DG)) \geqslant -n - c\). One transforms this relation
using the isomorphism \(R^{!} f(DG) \simeq D(R f_{*}(G))\) (SGA 5 I
1.12) and applying the local duality theorem at the point \(t\); one
obtains thus

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i(R f\_*(G))\_t = 0 for i \textgreater{} n + c,}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

which is none other than (ii).

\textbf{4.8.} The hypotheses being those of 4.4 with \(g\) proper (resp.
4.5, resp. 4.6 with \(g\) proper), if \(V\) is an open neighborhood of
\(Y\) in \(X\), the morphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i(V, F) → H\^{}i(Y, j\^{}* F)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

is bijective for \(i < n - c - 1\), injective for \(i = n - c - 1\). If
\(\iota : V \to X\) is the canonical morphism, it suffices indeed to see
that one applies 4.4 (resp. 4.5, resp. 4.6) to the complex
\(R \iota_{*}(F|_{V})\). One can ask whether the preceding morphism is
bijective for \(i = n - c - 1\), injective for \(i = n - c\). It
evidently suffices for the hypotheses to be verified when one replaces
\(n\) by \(n + 1\); the proposition that follows shows that it suffices
to require a little less.

\textbf{Proposition 4.9.}

Let \(S\) be a local excellent scheme of characteristic zero with closed
point \(t\) (resp. in addition to the preceding conditions, one supposes
\(S\) henselian), \(f : X \to S\) a scheme proper over \(S\) (resp.
proper over \(S - {t}\)), and \(U\) an open set of \(X\) union of
\(c + 1\) affine opens. Let \(Y\) be a closed subscheme of \(X\) with
underlying space \(X - U\), \(j : Y \to X\) the canonical morphism,
\(F\) a complex of sheaves of \(\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}\)-modules on
\(X\), bounded below with constructible cohomology, and \(n\) an
integer. Suppose that one has, for every point \(u\) of \(U\),

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{prof\_u(F) ⩾ inf(n − 1, n − δ\_t(u))  (resp. prof\_u(F) ⩾ inf(n − 1, n + 1 − δ\_t(u))).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Then for every open neighborhood \(V\) of \(Y\) in \(X\), the canonical
morphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i\_\{f\^{}\{−1\}(t)\}(V, F) → H\^{}i\_\{f\^{}\{−1\}(t) ∩ Y\}(Y, j\^{}* F)   (resp. H\^{}i(V, F) → H\^{}i(Y, j\^{}* F))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

is bijective for \(i < n - c - 2\) and injective for \(i = n - c - 2\).
Moreover, there exists an open neighborhood \texttt{V\_0} of \(Y\) in
\(X\) such that, for every other such \(V\) with \(V \subset V_{0}\),
the canonical morphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i\_\{f\^{}\{−1\}(t) ∩ V\}(V, F) → H\^{}i\_\{f\^{}\{−1\}(t) ∩ Y\}(Y, j\^{}* F)   (resp. H\^{}i(V, F) → H\^{}i(Y, j\^{}* F))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

is bijective for \(i < n - c - 1\), injective for \(i = n - c - 1\).

\emph{Proof.} Let us set, for simplicity,
\(\delta_{t'}(u) = \delta_{t}(u)\) (resp.
\(\delta_{t'}(u) = \delta_{t}(u) - 1\)). One deduces from 4.8 the first
assertion of 4.9, since the hypotheses of 4.4 (resp. 4.5) are verified
when one replaces \(n\) by \(n - 1\). They are also verified for \(n\)
itself, except at the points \(u\) such that \(\delta_{t'}(u) = 0\).
Now, for \(u
\in U\), to say that \(\delta_{t'}(u) = 0\) is equivalent to saying that
\(u\) is a closed point of \(U_{t}\) (resp. a closed point of \(X\)).
Let \(E\) be the set of points of \(U\) such that
\(\delta_{t'}(u) = 0\); let us show that, for all the points
\(u \in E\), except a finite number, one has
\(prof_{u}(F) \geqslant n\). Let \(\bar{S}\) be the strict localization
of \(S\) at \(t\), \(S'\) the completion of \(\bar{S}\) with closed
point \(t'\), and consider the cartesian square

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{U′ {-}{-}h{-}{-}\textgreater{} U}
\NormalTok{|         |}
\NormalTok{f′        f}
\NormalTok{|         |}
\NormalTok{v         v}
\NormalTok{S′ {-}{-}g{-}{-}\textgreater{} S.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The depth hypotheses at the points of \(U\) are preserved when one
replaces \(U\) by \(U'\) and \(F\) by the inverse image \(F'\) of \(F\)
on \(U'\). Indeed let \(u' \in U'\) and \(u = h(u')\). If
\(u \notin E\), one has the relation \(prof_{u}(F) \geqslant n -
\delta_{t'}(u)\), and it follows from 4.2.1 that this entails the
relation \(prof_{u'}(F') \geqslant n - \delta_{t'}(u')\). If
\(u \in E\), \(u'\) is a closed point of \(U'_{t}\) (resp. a closed
point of \(X' = X \times_{S} S'\)), and since the fiber \(U'_{u}\) of
\(h\) at \(u\) is of dimension zero and \(h\) regular, it follows from
1.16 that one has \(prof_{u'}(F') =
prof_{u}(F) \geqslant n - 1\).

Let then \(K\) be a dualizing complex on \(S'\), normalized at the
closed point \(t'\), and \(DF'\) the dual of \(F'\) with respect to
\(R^{!} f(K)\). By 4.2.2, the étale depth hypotheses at the points of
\(U'\) translate by the relations:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(H\^{}q(DF′))\_\{ū′\} = 0 for q \textgreater{} −n − δ\_\{t′\}(u′)  (resp. q \textgreater{} −n − 2 − δ\_\{t′\}(u′)),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

if \(u'\) is not a point of \(E' = h^{-1}(E)\),

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(H\^{}q(DF′))\_\{ū′\} = 0 for q \textgreater{} −(n − 1)  (resp. q \textgreater{} −n − 1), if u′ ∈ E′.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Let \(G = H^{-(n - 1)}(DF')\) (resp. \(G = H^{-n - 1}(DF')\)); since
\(G\) is a constructible sheaf, the set of points at which the geometric
fiber is non-null is a constructible set (SGA 4 IX 2.4 (iv)); now by
hypothesis this set is contained in the set \(E'\) of closed points of
\(U'_{t'}\) (resp. of points of \(U'\) closed in \(X'\)); it therefore
follows from 4.9.1 below that this set reduces to a finite number of
points. Applying 4.2.2, one sees that, for all the points of \(E'\)
except a finite number, one has \(prof_{u'}(F') \geqslant n\). It
follows by 1.16 that, for all the points of \(E\) except a finite
number, one has

\[ prof_{u}(F) \geqslant n. \]

Let \(V\) be an open neighborhood of \(Y\) in \(X\), contained in the
complement in \(X\) of the finite set of points \(u\) of \(E\) for which
one has \(prof_{u}(F) = n - 1\). If \(\iota : V \to X\) is the canonical
immersion, let

\[ F_{1} = R \iota_{*}(F|_{V}); \]

then \texttt{F\_1} is a complex of sheaves on \(X\) with constructible
cohomology (SGA 4 XIX 5.1) and bounded below. We shall see that, for
every point \(u\) of \(U\), the complex \texttt{F\_1} verifies the
relation

\[ (\ast) prof_{u}(F_{1}) \geqslant n - \delta_{t'}(u). \]

If \(u \in U \cap V\), one has \(prof_{u}(F_{1}) = prof_{u}(F)\), and
the relation (∗) is verified by hypothesis at the points of \(U\) not
belonging to \(E\); for the latter, it is also verified by the choice of
\(V\). Finally, if \(u \in U\) and \(u \notin V\), one has by 1.6 g)
\(prof_{u}(F_{1}) = \infty\). One then applies 4.4 (resp. 4.5) replacing
\(F\) by \texttt{F\_1}; one obtains the announced result, taking into
account that one has, for every \(i\):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i\_\{f\^{}\{−1\}(t)\}(X, R ι\_*(F|\_V)) ≃ H\^{}i\_\{f\^{}\{−1\}(t) ∩ V\}(V, F)  (resp. H\^{}i(X, R ι\_*(F|\_V)) ≃ H\^{}i(V, F)).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Lemma 4.9.1.}

A constructible set \(E\) contained in the set of closed points of a
noetherian scheme \(X\)

is reduced to a finite number of points.

Indeed, \(E\) is a finite union of sets of the form
\(U \cap \complement V\), where \(U\) and \(V\) are open sets of \(X\);
by hypothesis all the points of \(U \cap \complement V\) are maximal
points of this set, hence they are finite in number.

\section{5. Geometrical depth}\label{geometrical-depth}

To apply 4.2 and its corollaries in practice, one needs a convenient
criterion to verify the étale-depth hypotheses at the points of \(U\).
We shall give such a criterion, using the local Lefschetz theorem 4.5.

\textbf{5.1.} Let \(A\) be a noetherian local ring; when we speak of the
étale depth of \(A\), this will mean the depth at the closed point. We
are going to introduce a notion of ``geometrical depth of \(A\)'', and
use 4.5 to compare it to the étale depth \(prof^{\acute{e}}t(A)\).

\textbf{Proposition 5.2.}

Let \(A\) be a noetherian local ring; suppose that \(A\) is isomorphic
to a quotient of a regular local ring \(B\) by an ideal \(I\) (this is
true, for example, when \(A\) is complete by virtue of the Cohen theorem
(\href{https://jcreinhold.github.io/ega/iv/06-ch0-19-formally-smooth-algebras.html\#198-cohen-algebras-and-p-cohen-rings-application-to-the-structure-of-complete-local-rings}{EGA
0\_IV 19.8.8})). Let \(q\) be the minimal number of generators of \(I\);
then the number \(\dim(B) - q\) is independent of the choice of \(B\).

The minimal number of generators of \(I\) is also equal to the rank of
the \(k\)-vector space \(I \otimes_{B} k\), where \(k\) denotes the
residue field of \(A\). One reduces immediately to the case where \(A\)
is complete, since one has \(\hat{A}
\simeq \hat{B}/\hat{I}\) with \(\dim \hat{B} = \dim B\) and
\(rg_{k}(I \otimes_{B} k) = rg_{k}(\hat{I} \otimes_{\hat{B}}
k)\); for the same reason one can suppose \(B\) complete.

Let \(B\) and \(B'\) be two complete regular local rings,
\(f : B \to A\), \(f' : B' \to A\) two surjective homomorphisms, and
\(I = Ker(f)\), \(I' = Ker(f')\). One must show that

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{dim B − rg\_k(I ⊗\_B k) = dim B′ − rg\_k(I′ ⊗\_\{B′\} k).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Let us first place ourselves in the case where one has a factorization
of the form

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{B {-}{-}f{-}{-}\textgreater{} A}
\NormalTok{ \textbackslash{}      /}
\NormalTok{  g    f′}
\NormalTok{   \textbackslash{}  /}
\NormalTok{    B′}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

with \(g\) surjective. Let \(J = Ker(g)\); then \(J \subset I\) and
\(I/J = I'\). Since \(B'\) is regular, \(\dim(B') = \dim(B) -
rg_{k}(J \otimes_{B} k)\) and \(J\) is generated by elements forming
part of a regular system of parameters of \(B\). It follows that one has
the exact sequence

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{0 → J ⊗\_B k → I ⊗\_B k → J/I ⊗\_\{B′\} k → 0,}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

and consequently

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{dim B − rg\_k(I ⊗\_B k) = dim B − rg\_k(J ⊗\_B k) − rg\_k(J/I ⊗\_\{B′\} k) = dim B′ − rg\_k(I′ ⊗\_\{B′\} k).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The general case reduces to the preceding; to see this, it suffices to
show that one can find a complete regular local ring \(B''\) and
surjective homomorphisms \(g : B'' \to B\) and \(g' : B'' \to B'\),
rendering commutative the diagram

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{                  B}
\NormalTok{                ↗    ↘ f}
\NormalTok{              g        \textbackslash{}}
\NormalTok{(∗)      B′′            A}
\NormalTok{              g′       /}
\NormalTok{                ↘    ↗ f′}
\NormalTok{                  B′.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Now, if \(W\) is a Cohen ring with residue field \(k\), one has a local
morphism \(W \to A\) that lifts to \(B\) and \(B'\)
(\href{https://jcreinhold.github.io/ega/iv/32-ch4-19-regular-immersions.html\#198-properties-of-passage-to-projective-limit}{EGA
IV 19.8.6}), so that one has the commutative diagram

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{                  B}
\NormalTok{                ↗}
\NormalTok{               /}
\NormalTok{          W              A}
\NormalTok{               \textbackslash{}}
\NormalTok{                ↘}
\NormalTok{                  B′.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

One can find integers \(n\) and \(n'\) and surjective morphisms
\(h : W[[T_{1}, \cdots, T_{n}]] \to B\) and \(h' : W[[T'_{1},
\cdots, T'_{n'}]] \to B'\) that are morphisms of \(W\)-algebras (EGA
0\_IV 19.8.8); if one then sets \(B'' = W[[T_{1},
\cdots, T_{n}, T'_{1}, \cdots, T'_{n'}]]\) and if one defines \(g\) and
\(g'\) as morphisms of \(W\)-algebras such that

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{g(T\_i) = h(T\_i),  g(T′\_i) = b\_i,  g′(T\_i) = b′\_i,  g′(T′\_i) = h′(T′\_i),}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

where \(b_{i}\) (resp. \(b'_{i}\)) is an element of \(B\) (resp. of
\(B'\)) lifting \((f' \circ h')(T'_{i})\) (resp. \((f \circ
h)(T_{i})\)), the diagram (∗) is indeed commutative.

Proposition 5.2 justifies the following definition:

\textbf{Definition 5.3.}

Let \(A\) be a noetherian local ring, \texttt{Â} its completion, which
is therefore isomorphic to the quotient of a complete regular local ring
\(B\) by an ideal \(I\); if \(q\) is the minimal number of generators of
\(I\), one calls geometrical depth of \(A\) the number

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{prof.géom(A) = dim B − q.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Proposition 5.4.}

Let \(A\) be a noetherian local ring. Then one has

\[ prof.g\acute{e}om(A) \leqslant \dim A, \]

and one has equality if and only if \(A\) is a complete intersection.

One can suppose \(A\) complete. Let then \(A = B/I\), where \(B\) is a
complete regular local ring and \(I\) an ideal of \(B\). If
\((x_{1}, \cdots, x_{q})\) is a minimal system of generators of \(I\),
one has \(\dim A \geqslant \dim B - q\), and to say that
\(\dim A = \dim B - q\) is equivalent to saying that
\((x_{1}, \cdots, x_{q})\) forms part of a system of parameters of \(B\)
(\href{https://jcreinhold.github.io/ega/iv/03-ch0-16-dimension-and-depth.html\#163-systems-of-parameters-in-a-noetherian-local-ring}{EGA
0\_IV 16.3.7}); the proposition follows immediately.

\textbf{Proposition 5.5.}

Let \(A\) and \(A'\) be noetherian local rings, \(f : A \to A'\) a local
homomorphism. Suppose that \(f\) is flat and that, denoting by \(k\) the
residue field of \(A\), \(A' \otimes_{A} k\) is a field, a separable
extension of \(k\). Then one has

\[ prof.g\acute{e}om(A) = prof.g\acute{e}om(A'). \]

By replacing \(A\) and \(A'\) by their completions, one can suppose
\(A\) and \(A'\) complete (it follows from
(\href{https://jcreinhold.github.io/ega/iii/03-ch0-10-complements-flat-modules.html\#102-local-criteria-of-flatness}{EGA
0\_III 10.2.1}) that the flatness hypothesis is preserved and this is
evident for the other hypotheses). Let then \(A = B/I\), where \(B\) is
a regular local ring and \(I\) an ideal of \(B\). Since \(A'\) is
formally smooth over \(A\)
(\href{https://jcreinhold.github.io/ega/iv/06-ch0-19-formally-smooth-algebras.html\#198-cohen-algebras-and-p-cohen-rings-application-to-the-structure-of-complete-local-rings}{EGA
0\_IV 19.8.2}), it follows from
(\href{https://jcreinhold.github.io/ega/iv/06-ch0-19-formally-smooth-algebras.html\#197-case-of-local-homomorphisms-existence-and-uniqueness-theorems}{EGA
0\_IV 19.7.2}) that one can find a complete noetherian local ring \(B'\)
and a local homomorphism \(B \to
B'\) such that \(B'\) is a flat \(B\)-module and
\(B' \otimes_{B} A \simeq A'\). One therefore has \(A' \simeq B'/IB'\);
moreover the ring \(B'\) is regular; indeed, if \(\mathfrak{m}\) is the
maximal ideal of \(B\), \(\mathfrak{m} B'\) is the maximal ideal of
\(B'\), and since \(\mathfrak{m}\) is generated by a regular sequence by
definition of ``regular'', \(\mathfrak{m} B'\) is generated by a
\(B'\)-regular sequence
(\href{https://jcreinhold.github.io/ega/iv/02-ch0-15-mf-regular-sequences.html\#151-m-regular-sequences-and-m-quasi-regular-sequences}{EGA
0\_IV 15.1.14}). Since one evidently has \(\dim B = \dim B'\), and since
\(I\) and \(IB'\) have the same minimal number of generators, the
assertion follows.

\textbf{Theorem 5.6.}\footnote{Editors' note: Illusie has since shown
  the inequality \(prof_{x}(\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{\nu} \mathbb{Z})
  \geqslant prof.g\acute{e}om_{x}(X/S) - \delta(x) + 1\) for \(x\) a
  point of \(X\) a scheme of finite type over a trait \(S\) of residual
  characteristic prime to \(\ell\), and \(\nu \geqslant 1\). If \(S\) is
  of characteristic zero, this is a consequence of theorem 5.6; see
  (Illusie L., ``Perversité et variation'', Manuscripta Math. 112
  (2003), p.~271-295).}

Let \(A\) be an excellent local ring of characteristic zero. Then one
has

\[ prof^{\acute{e}}t(A) \geqslant prof.g\acute{e}om(A). \]

One can suppose \(A\) strictly local complete, since the geometrical
depth and the étale depth are preserved by passage to the strict
henselization and to the completion by 5.5 and 1.16. Let
\(A \simeq B/I\), where \(B\) is a complete regular local ring, and let
\((f_{1}, \cdots, f_{q})\) be a minimal system of generators of the
ideal \(I\). One therefore has

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{π = prof.géom(A) = dim B − q.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Consider the closed immersion

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Y = Spec A → X = Spec B,}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

and let \(U = X - Y = \bigcup_{1 \leqslant i \leqslant q} X_{f_{i}}\).
If \(a\) denotes the closed point of \(X\), one must show that, for
every prime number \(p\), one has

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i\_a(Y, ℤ/pℤ) = 0 for i \textless{} π.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Since \(B\) is regular excellent, one has
\(prof^{\acute{e}}t(B) = 2 \dim X\) (cf.~1.10) and consequently
\(H^{i}_{a}(X,
\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}) = 0\) for \(i < 2 \dim X\). To prove the
theorem, it therefore suffices to show that the morphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(∗) H\^{}i\_a(X, ℤ/pℤ) → H\^{}i\_a(Y, ℤ/pℤ)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

is bijective for \(i < \pi\). One applies for this the local Lefschetz
theorem 4.5 with \(n = \pi + q - 1\), \(c = q - 1\), so \(n - c = \pi\).
Note that \(U = X - Y\) is the union of the \(q\) affine opens
\(X_{f_{i}}\). Let us show that one has, for every point \(u\) of \(U\):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{prof\^{}ét\_u(X) ⩾ π + q − 1 − dim(\{u\}) = dim O\_\{X,u\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

(where \({u}\) denotes the closure of \(u\) in \(X - {a}\)). Indeed it
follows from 1.10 that one has

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{prof\^{}ét\_u(X) = 2 dim O\_\{X,u\} ⩾ dim O\_\{X,u\}.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Using 4.5, one sees that (∗) is bijective for \(i < \pi\), which
completes the proof

of the theorem.

\textbf{Corollary 5.7.}

Let \(S\) be the spectrum of a field of characteristic zero (resp. an
excellent henselian local scheme of characteristic zero),
\(f : X \to S\) a scheme proper over \(S\) (resp. over \(S - {s}\)). Let
\(U\) be a union of \(c + 1\) affine opens of \(X\), \(Y\) a closed
subscheme with underlying space \(X - U\), \(n\) and \(m\) positive
integers. Suppose that, for every point \(u\) of \(U\), one has

\[ prof.g\acute{e}om(O_{X,u}) \geqslant n - \dim({u}) \]

(\({u}\) closure of \(u\) in \(X\)). Then the canonical morphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H\^{}i(X, ℤ/mℤ) → H\^{}i(Y, ℤ/mℤ)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

is bijective for \(i < n - c - 1\), injective for \(i = n - c - 1\).

One applies 4.5 and 4.6. The étale-depth hypotheses at the points of
\(U\) are verified, since by 5.6

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{prof\^{}ét\_u(X) ⩾ prof.géom(O\_\{X,u\}) ⩾ n − dim(\{u\}).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{6. Open questions}\label{open-questions}

\textbf{6.1.} One can ask whether the implication (ii) ⇒ (i) of 4.2 is
valid more generally for torsion sheaves \(F\), not necessarily
annihilated by a given integer \(m\)

and not necessarily constructible. In the case where \(S\) is not of
characteristic zero, it seems possible that this implication remains
valid, even for \(p\)-torsion sheaves (\(p\) the residual
characteristic). Finally it is not clear either that the hypothesis
\(S\) excellent cannot be lifted.

\textbf{6.2.} Let \(X\) be a scheme proper over a field \(k\), or the
complement of the closed point of a henselian local scheme, and
\(j : Y \to X\) a closed subscheme of \(X\) whose complement \(U\) is
affine. Then, if \(F\) is a sheaf of sets on \(X\) or a sheaf of
not-necessarily-commutative groups, the statements 4.5 or 4.6 and 4.9
still have a meaning for such an \(F\), provided one restricts to small
values of \(n\). If \(u\) is a point of \(U\), one denotes by \texttt{ū}
a geometric point above \(u\), by \(X_{(u)}\) the strict localization of
\(X\) at \texttt{ū}, and by \(F_{u}\) the fiber of \(F\) at \texttt{ū}.
Then, by making possibly certain hypotheses on \(X\) and on \(F\), for
example by supposing \(X\) excellent (possibly of characteristic zero,
or of equal characteristic by using resolution of singularities) and
\(F\) ind-finite (or if needed even \(L\)-ind-finite with \(L\) prime to
the characteristic of \(X\)), one would like to prove the following
statements:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi})}
\tightlist
\item
  Let \(F\) be a sheaf of sets (resp. a sheaf of groups) and suppose
  that, for every point \(u\) of \(U\), one has
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{F\_u → H⁰(X\_\{(u)\} − ū, F)  injective if dim(\{u\}) ⩽ 1}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

(that is, for such a \(u\), one has \(prof_{u}(F) \geqslant 1\)). Then,
when \(V\) runs over the set

of open neighborhoods of \(Y\), the canonical morphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{lim → \_V H⁰(V, F) → H⁰(Y, j\^{}* F)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

is bijective (resp. one has the preceding conclusion and moreover the
morphism \(\lim \to {}_{V} H^{1}(V, F) \to H^{1}(Y,
j^{*} F)\) is injective). If \(F\) is constructible, one can replace the
\(\lim \to\) by the cohomology of \(V\) for \(V\) ``small enough''.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi})}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  Let \(F\) be a sheaf of sets (resp. a sheaf of groups) and suppose
  that, for every point \(u\) of \(U\), one has
  \(prof_{u}(F) \geqslant 2 - \dim({u})\), which translates also by the
  relations
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{F\_u → H⁰(X\_\{(u)\} − ū, F) is bijective if dim(\{u\}) = 0}
\NormalTok{F\_u → H⁰(X\_\{(u)\} − ū, F) is injective if dim(\{u\}) = 1.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Then the canonical morphism

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H⁰(X, F) → H⁰(Y, j\^{}* F)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

is bijective (resp. one has the preceding conclusion and moreover the
morphism \(H^{1}(X, F) \to H^{1}(Y, j^{*} F)\) is injective).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi})}
\setcounter{enumi}{2}
\tightlist
\item
  Let \(F\) be an ind-finite sheaf of groups. Suppose that, for every
  point \(u\) of \(U\), one has
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{F\_u → H⁰(X\_\{(u)\} − ū, F) bijective if dim(\{u\}) = 0 or 1,}
\NormalTok{F\_u → H⁰(X\_\{(u)\} − ū, F) injective if dim(\{u\}) = 2.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Then, when \(V\) runs over the set of open neighborhoods of \(Y\), the
canonical

morphisms

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{lim → \_V H⁰(V, F) → H⁰(Y, j\^{}* F)  and  lim → \_V H¹(V, F) → H¹(Y, j\^{}* F)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

are bijective. If \(F\) is constructible, one can replace the
\(\lim \to\) by the cohomology of \(V\) for \(V\) small enough.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi})}
\setcounter{enumi}{3}
\tightlist
\item
  Let \(F\) be a sheaf of groups. Suppose that, for every point \(u\) of
  \(U\), one has \(prof_{u}(F) \geqslant 3 -
  \dim({u})\), which translates also by the conditions
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{F\_u → H⁰(X\_\{(u)\} − ū, F) bijective, and H¹(X\_\{(u)\} − ū, F) = 0 if dim(\{u\}) = 0,}
\NormalTok{F\_u → H⁰(X\_\{(u)\} − ū, F) bijective if dim(\{u\}) = 1,}
\NormalTok{F\_u → H⁰(X\_\{(u)\} − ū, F) injective if dim(\{u\}) = 2.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Then the canonical morphisms

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{H⁰(X, F) → H⁰(Y, j\^{}* F)  and  H¹(X, F) → H¹(Y, j\^{}* F)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

are bijective.

As an indication in favor of these statements\footnote{Editors' note:
  all the statements of 6.2, apart from the constructible variants, have
  been proved by Mme Raynaud; see (Raynaud M., ``Théorèmes de Lefschetz
  en cohomologie des faisceaux cohérents et en cohomologie étale.
  Application au groupe fondamental'', Ann. Sci. Éc. Norm. Sup. (4) 7
  (1974), p.~29-52, corollary III.1.3).}, we mention XIII 2.1, X 3.4 and
XII 3.5. Note that, thanks to the argument of 4.8 and 4.9, statement a)
(resp. c)) would follow from b) (resp. d)).

\textbf{6.3.} From d) would follow the statement, analogous to 5.6: if
\(A\) is a noetherian local ring (possibly excellent) and if
\(prof.g\acute{e}om(A) \geqslant 3\), then one has

\(prof^{hop}(A) \geqslant 3\). To see this, one realizes
\(Y' = \operatorname{Spec} A\) as a closed subset of a regular local
scheme \(X' = \operatorname{Spec} B\), whose complement is a union of
\(q\) affine opens, with the relation \(\dim B -
q = prof.g\acute{e}om(A)\). One has, for every point \(x\) of \(X'\), if
\(n = \dim B\),
\texttt{prof\^{}\{hop\}\_x(X)\ ⩾\ inf(3,\ n\ −\ dim(\{x\}))} (cf.~1.11),
and one deduces from d) that this entails
\texttt{prof\^{}\{hop\}\_y(Y′)\ ⩾\ inf(3,\ n\ −\ q\ −\ dim(\{y\}))} for
every point \(y\) of \(Y'\). The result is obtained then by taking for
\(y\) the closed point of \(Y'\).

\textbf{6.4.} A variant of 4.2, at least of the implication (ii) ⇒ (i),
should still be valid in the complex analytic case, provided one works
with ``analytically constructible'' sheaves (cf.~XIII); the proof would
be analogous to that of 4.2, using the duality theory of J.-L. Verdier.
Note, on the other hand, that for the complex analytic analogue of the
non-commutative variants signalled in 6.2, one does not even have a
method of attack for the statements concerning the fundamental group
suggested by the results of Exposés X, XII, XIII recalled at the end of
6.2. The methods of the Séminaire indeed seem irremediably tied to the
case of finite coverings (which can be studied in terms of coherent
sheaves of algebras).

\section{Footnotes}\label{footnotes-2}

\chapter{Glossary and Translation Ledger --- SGA
2}\label{glossary-and-translation-ledger-sga-2}

This file records the translation choices made for the SGA 2 Markdown
translation. It is the authoritative reference for the parallel
translators of individual Exposés; every translator must consult it
before drafting and must obey it where it applies.

chaque fois que & whenever & Standard. \\

\section{Common OCR repairs}\label{common-ocr-repairs}

These recur across the source files. Apply them locally as the
translator reads each block; do not silently ``correct'' the mathematics
--- only repair what the OCR clearly mangled, and flag anything
genuinely ambiguous with a
\texttt{\textless{}!-\/-\ TRANSLATOR\ NOTE:\ ...\ -\/-\textgreater{}}
comment.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Dropped sub/superscripts on big operators.} \(\Gamma\),
  \(H^{i}\), \(Ext^{i}\), \(R^{i} f_{*}\), \texttt{O\_X},
  \(H^{\bullet}\), \(H_{\bullet}\) routinely lose their indices. The
  surrounding French sentence almost always names them (``la cohomologie
  locale de F le long de Y'', ``les \(H^{i}\) de F'', etc.); recover
  from context.
\item
  \textbf{Underline loss on sheafified functors.} SGA 2 distinguishes
  \(\Gamma Z\) (the global section functor --- non-underlined) from
  \(\Gamma Z\) (the sheafified functor --- underlined). The OCR
  routinely loses the underline. In the translation, render the
  sheafified functor with a script-H \(\mathcal{H}\) for the cohomology
  version (\(\mathcal{H}^{i}_{Y}(F)\)) and keep an explicit \(\Gamma Z\)
  for the underlined section functor, pinning the convention at first
  use per Exposé with a small note.
\item
  \textbf{Hat across line break.} Formal-completion hats break across
  lines: \(Et( b / X)\) is \(\hat{E}t(\hat{X})\). \texttt{X\ b} after a
  closing paren is \(\hat{X}\). Repair as needed.
\item
  \textbf{OCR substitutions for arrows.}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \(7\to\) is \(\mapsto\).
  \item
    \(-\to\) is \(\to\).
  \item
    \(\sim=\) (often broken across lines) is \(\cong\).
  \item
    \texttt{\textbackslash{}sim\textbackslash{}n\textbackslash{}to} (OCR
    artefact, with literal newline) is \(\xrightarrow{\sim}\) or \(\to\)
    annotated with \texttt{\textasciitilde{}}; pick the rendering that
    matches what is being claimed.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{OCR substitutions for accents.}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \(Et(X)\) should be \(\hat{E}t(X)\) (the étale site / category of
    étale objects).
  \item
    \texttt{etale} should be \texttt{étale}.
  \item
    \texttt{Tohoku} should be \emph{Tôhoku}.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Greek subscripts.} \(\pi 1\), \(\pi 0\), \texttt{H1},
  \texttt{H0} etc. should be \(\pi_{1}\), \(\pi_{0}\), \(H^{1}\),
  \(H^{0}\) etc. (Unicode), inside backticks.
\item
  \textbf{Math run-ons.} When a displayed equation has been broken into
  prose by the OCR, restore it as a fenced
  \texttt{\textasciigrave{}\textasciigrave{}\textasciigrave{}text}
  block. - \textbf{Embedded N.D.E. footnotes.} Source convention is
  \texttt{(N)\ N.D.E.\ :\ \textless{}text\textgreater{}} at the bottom
  of a page. Recover the whole footnote (which may span lines) and
  render it as \texttt{{[}\^{}N.D.E-N{]}} (or
  \texttt{{[}\^{}N.D.E-\textless{}expose\textgreater{}-\textless{}n\textgreater{}{]}}
  if multiple Exposés might collide) with its English translation in the
  footnote body. - \textbf{Dash lead-ins.} Statement bodies preceded by
  \texttt{—} in the source (after \texttt{Proposition\ X.Y.},
  \(Th\acute{e}or\grave{e}me
  X.Y.\), etc.) are rendered SGA 1-style: bold keyword on its own line,
  then a paragraph break, then the body.
\end{itemize}

\section{Style anchors (style is non-negotiable for cross-volume
consistency)}\label{style-anchors-style-is-non-negotiable-for-cross-volume-consistency}

These are inherited from SGA 1's translation; deviate only if SGA 1
itself is inconsistent.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{File naming.} \texttt{00-title-preface.md},
  \texttt{00-introduction.md},
  \texttt{NN-\textless{}slug\textgreater{}.md} for Exposés,
  \texttt{glossary.md}, \texttt{zz-index-*.md} for indexes.
\item
  \textbf{Heading hierarchy.}
  \texttt{\#\ Exposé\ N.\ \textless{}English\ Title\textgreater{}}, then
  \texttt{\#\#\ 1.\ \textless{}Section\ Title\textgreater{}}, then
  \texttt{\#\#\#\ \textless{}Subsection\textgreater{}} if needed. The
  Exposé heading carries a
  \texttt{\textless{}!-\/-\ label:\ N\ -\/-\textgreater{}} comment on
  the next line.
\item
  \textbf{Statement blocks.} \texttt{**Proposition.**},
  \texttt{**Lemma.**}, \texttt{**Theorem.**}, \texttt{**Corollary.**},
  \texttt{**Definition.**}, \texttt{**Remark.**}, \texttt{**Example.**}
  on their own line. The label
  \texttt{\textless{}!-\/-\ label:\ N.X.Y\ -\/-\textgreater{}} follows
  on the next line (after a blank line). The body begins after another
  blank line.
\item
  \textbf{Math.} Unicode math wrapped in backticks for inline. Displayed
  math in fenced
  \texttt{\textasciigrave{}\textasciigrave{}\textasciigrave{}text}
  blocks. Numbered displayed equations get a
  \texttt{\textless{}!-\/-\ label:\ eq:N.X.Y\ -\/-\textgreater{}}
  comment immediately after the closing fence.
\item
  \textbf{Footnotes.} Markdown \texttt{{[}\^{}slug{]}} syntax. The
  footnote body goes at the end of the section (or end of file).
  Original Grothendieck-era footnotes:
  \texttt{{[}\^{}\textless{}exposenum\textgreater{}-\textless{}sec\textgreater{}-\textless{}n\textgreater{}{]}}
  slugs (e.g.~\texttt{{[}\^{}I-1-1{]}}). Editor footnotes:
  \texttt{{[}\^{}N.D.E-\textless{}expose\textgreater{}-\textless{}n\textgreater{}{]}}
  (e.g.~\texttt{{[}\^{}N.D.E-IV-2{]}}).
\item
  \textbf{Page marks.}
  \texttt{\textless{}!-\/-\ original\ page\ N\ -\/-\textgreater{}} on
  its own line at page boundaries.
\item
  \textbf{Cross-references.} Use the source's own convention:
  \texttt{(VIII\ 2.3)}, \texttt{(EGA\ III\ 4.1.5)},
  \texttt{(SGA\ 4\ IV\ 3.8)}.
\item
  \textbf{Bibliographies.} Each Exposé's own bibliography section, at
  its end, is preserved as a \texttt{\#\#\ Bibliography} section with
  entries as a numbered list, journal titles in italics, with the
  original keys (e.g.~\texttt{{[}1{]}}, \texttt{{[}2{]}}) preserved as
  leading bracketed labels.
\end{itemize}
